Reliable Explanations or Random Noise? A Reliability Metric for XAI
Poushali Sengupta, Sabita Maharjan, Frank Eliassen, Shashi Raj Pandey, Yan Zhang

Abstract
In recent years, explaining decisions made by complex machine learning models has become essential in high-stakes domains such as energy systems, healthcare, finance, and autonomous systems. However, the reliability of these explanations, namely, whether they remain stable and consistent under realistic, non-adversarial changes, remains largely unmeasured. Widely used methods such as SHAP and Integrated Gradients (IG) are well-motivated by axiomatic notions of attribution, yet their explanations can vary substantially even under system-level conditions, including small input perturbations, correlated representations, and minor model updates. Such variability undermines explanation reliability, as reliable explanations should remain consistent across equivalent input representations and small, performance-preserving model changes. We introduce the Explanation Reliability Index (ERI), a family of metrics that quantifies explanation stability under four reliability axioms: robustness to small input perturbations, consistency under feature redundancy, smoothness across model evolution, and resilience to mild distributional shifts. For each axiom, we derive formal guarantees, including Lipschitz-type bounds and temporal stability results. We further propose ERI-T, a dedicated measure of temporal reliability for sequential models, and introduce ERI-Bench, a benchmark designed to systematically stress-test explanation reliability across synthetic and real-world datasets. Experimental results reveal widespread reliability failures in popular explanation methods, showing that explanations can be unstable under realistic deployment conditions. By exposing and quantifying these instabilities, ERI enables principled assessment of explanation reliability and supports more trustworthy explainable AI (XAI) systems.



1
Introduction

As transparency, accountability, and safety have become critical requirements, XAI has emerged as an essential component for deploying complex machine learning models. Techniques such as SHAP
(
23
)
, IG
(
31
)
, DeepLIFT
(
28
)
, SAGE
(
9
)
, and perturbation-based importance scores
(
16
)
are widely used to interpret predictions in healthcare, finance, climate modeling, and scientific discovery. Despite their broad adoption, a fundamental question remains unanswered:
How reliable are these explanations?
Most existing evaluation tools focus on predictive accuracy or agreement with synthetic ground truth, assessing how well explanations align with a model’s output behavior. However, strong predictive performance alone does not guarantee that explanations are reliable or stable under realistic variations. An explanation may appear reasonable on average yet remain highly unstable under small, non-adversarial variations such as small input noise, correlated features, minor model checkpoint changes, or mild distributional shifts. Prior studies have documented these failures extensively
(
2
)
: small perturbations can alter attribution rankings, redundant features can distort Shapley values, and minor training variations can cause large oscillations in explanations. Intuitively, redundant features should improve explanation robustness by preserving the same semantic information, for example, representing temperature in both Celsius and Fahrenheit should not change which factor is deemed important, yet many existing explanation methods treat such features independently and arbitrarily redistribute importance, leading to increased instability
(
21
;
7
)
. Such instability undermines trust and limits the practical usefulness of explainable machine learning systems. Moreover, to the best of our knowledge, there exists no metric for assessing explanation reliability across different small, non-adversarial variations.

Real-world data, such as meteorological measurements, medical signals, or sensor
readings, inevitably contain noise and correlations
(
26
)
.
A reliable explanation should satisfy all four reliability axioms
simultaneously, each reflecting a practical deployment challenge.
Stability under small, non-adversarial perturbations (A1)
requires
explanations to remain unchanged under small, non-adversarial input
variations
(
2
)
.
Redundancy-collapse consistency (A2)
ensures that semantically equivalent
or highly correlated features (e.g., height in inches versus centimeters)
receive consistent attribution
(
1
)
.
Model-evolution consistency (A3)
requires explanations to remain stable
across model retraining or updates when predictive behavior changes a
little
(
32
;
15
)
.
Distributional and temporal robustness (A4)
demands that explanations
remain reliable under natural data shifts over time or across sources, which are
common in vision, language, and time-series
applications
(
26
;
4
)
. Violations of any of these properties can render explanations unstable,
misleading, or difficult to compare, even when model predictions remain
unchanged. While existing explanation methods and evaluation protocols may satisfy one or
two of these properties in isolation, the literature lacks a quantitative metric
that captures these properties within a single, aggregated reliability
assessment
(
2
;
32
)
.
Most prior work relies on proxy criteria such as sparsity or agreement with
human intuition, providing no principled way to determine whether explanations
are reliable, comparable, and suitable for real-world deployment under small,
non-adversarial variations
(
10
)
.
As a result, explanation reliability is often evaluated qualitatively or through
ad hoc stress tests, and explanations that appear reliable in one setting may
fail in another
(
15
)
.
We argue that reliability is a core requirement of XAI: beyond producing
plausible explanations, XAI systems must provide information that can be
consistently relied upon over time
(
22
)
.
For example, a weather forecasting system becomes less dependable if small,
routine variations in input data lead to substantially different explanations,
even when predictions remain similar
(
26
)
.

To address this gap, we introduce the
Explanation Reliability Index (ERI)
family
, a set of model-agnostic, property-wise metrics that quantify how
consistently explanations reflect model behavior across multiple reliability
axioms, with an optional aggregation yielding a single summary score.
ERI is supported by strong theoretical foundations, including bounds on
explanation variation, guarantees for redundancy collapse, and a novel temporal
reliability measure for sequential models such as LSTMs, GRUs, Transformers, and
temporal CNNs
(
4
)
.
This temporal extension evaluates how smoothly explanations evolve over time,
independently of prediction smoothness, establishing ERI as a principled and
standardized reliability signal.
In addition, we present
ERI-Bench
, the first benchmark explicitly designed
to stress-test explanation reliability across vision, time-series, and tabular
data. ERI-Bench reveals substantial reliability failures in widely used explainers
across EEG microstates, UCI HAR, Norwegian load forecasting, and CIFAR-10,
showing that gradient-based and Shapley-based methods often suffer from
instability
(
23
;
31
)
, while dependence-aware
methods, such as MCIR (see Appendix
A
for details), Mutual
Information, and HSIC, achieve consistently higher reliability.
Together, ERI and ERI-Bench establish reliability as a fundamental dimension of
explanation quality and provide a principled foundation for the systematic
evaluation and improvement of XAI methods
1
1

1

Formal definitions, theoretical results, and extended experiments are provided in the appendix (Appendices
A
–
Z
). Code, datasets, and the ERI-Bench framework are available at
https://anonymous.4open.science/r/ERI-C316/README.md
.
. This work contributes to the field of XAI in three primary ways:

1.

Actionable Reliability Framework.

We introduce the ERI family, an axiomatic set of reliability measures that quantify explanation stability under realistic variations and enable practical uses such as reliability-aware checkpoint selection without degrading predictive performance.

2.

Temporal Reliability (ERI-T).

We propose ERI-T, the first quantitative metric for measuring how consistently explanations evolve over time in sequential models such as LSTMs, GRUs, Transformers, and temporal CNNs.

3.

ERI-Bench.

We release ERI-Bench, a benchmark designed to systematically evaluate explanation reliability under perturbations, feature correlations, model updates, and temporal variation, exposing widespread reliability failures in popular XAI methods.

2
Related Work

A wide range of explanation techniques assign importance scores to input features, including SHAP
(
23
)
, IG
(
31
)
, DeepLIFT
(
28
)
, SmoothGrad
(
30
)
, LIME
(
27
)
, and PFI
(
5
)
. Although these methods differ in how they approximate contributions, none provide a formal assessment of
reliability
under all four axiomatic properties stated before. Global importance measures such as SAGE
(
9
)
, global SHAP, and gradient-based saliency maps provide distribution-level insights, while dependence measures such as Mutual Information (MI), Conditional Mutual Information (CMI), and the Hilbert–Schmidt Independence Criterion (HSIC) quantify statistical association between inputs and model outputs. MI measures the overall dependence between variables, CMI captures dependence conditional on other features, and HSIC detects nonlinear dependence using kernel-based statistics
(
6
;
12
;
14
)
. Related measures such as CKA assess similarity between learned representations
(
19
)
. However, these do not evaluate whether explanations remain
stable
under small, non-adversarial variations. A growing body of work studies instability in explanations. Perturbation analyses show that many explainers are highly sensitive to input noise, with small changes substantially altering attribution rankings
(
13
;
18
)
. Redundancy and feature correlation further degrade reliability: SHAP is known to inflate attributions under correlated or duplicated features
(
21
;
8
)
, while gradient-based explainer outputs often change unpredictably. Existing fixes rely on heuristics rather than principled models of redundancy.Temporal robustness, namely, the requirement that explanations evolve smoothly over time when inputs and model behavior change gradually, has received limited attention. Most existing studies focus on prediction smoothness rather than explanation smoothness, and current saliency methods for sequential models
(
4
;
29
)
provide primarily qualitative insight without offering a quantitative measure of temporal reliability. In time-dependent systems such as forecasting, monitoring, or control, unstable explanations across consecutive time steps can obscure evolving system dynamics and undermine the practical usability of XAI, making temporal robustness a critical reliability requirement. Robustness under model changes and fine-tuning
(
32
)
, as well as structural modifications such as pruning and compression
(
15
)
, has been studied, but existing diagnostics remain fragmented and task-specific.

LIME is sensitive to sampling noise and violates perturbation stability (A1). SHAP satisfies efficiency and symmetry but fails under feature redundancy and distributional shifts (A2, A4). Gradient-based methods (e.g., IG, GradCAM++) show partial perturbation stability (A1) yet break under redundancy and temporal variation (A2, A4). Global dependence-based methods such as SAGE partially address feature correlation and redundancy (A2) but do not ensure consistency across model evolution (A3). We additionally report information-theoretic dependence measures, including MI and HSIC, as non-explanatory reference baselines that capture global statistical dependence but do not produce local attributions or assess temporal reliability. ERI addresses this gap through an aggregated, axiomatic framework for evaluating explanation reliability. ERI assigns a quantitative reliability score by evaluating attribution behavior against four axioms (A1–A4), with each component (ERI-S, ERI-R, ERI-M, ERI-D, and ERI-T) targeting a specific variation axis while remaining comparable within a single framework. Aggregating these components into a scalar score makes reliability explicit and directly comparable. ERI thus acts as a
verification layer
over existing explainers, complemented by ERI-Bench, the first benchmark designed to stress-test explanation reliability across synthetic, temporal, and real-world settings. To this end, this paper contributes a realistic and systematic approach to assessing the reliability of explanations.
ERI is not intended to replace faithfulness, correctness, or causal analysis, but to complement them by formalizing a general reliability functional over model-derived attribution signals, applicable beyond feature explanations to attention, saliency, influence functions, and representation-level analyses.

3
Axioms of Reliable Explanations

To reason about the reliability of explanation, we formalize four axioms that specify how a valid explanation method should behave under small and non-adversarial variations. Throughout this section, let
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
denote an input,
E(x)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
denote its explanation vector (e.g., attribution scores for each feature), and
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle d(\cdot,\cdot)$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle d(\cdot,\cdot)$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle d(\cdot,\cdot)$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle d(\cdot,\cdot)$}}}
.
2
2

2

Unless stated otherwise, theoretical results assume that

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle d$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle d$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle d$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle d$}}}
is a (pseudo-)metric, i.e., it satisfies symmetry and the triangle
inequality. Empirical evaluations may additionally report more general
non-metric dissimilarities (e.g., cosine or rank-based distances) as complementary
measures of directional or ordering changes.

be a non-negative dissimilarity between explanations. We use
\delta
to denote a perturbation applied to
x
,
\alpha
to represent a redundancy parameter, and
t
to index model checkpoints during training. Throughout this work, an explanation
E(x)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is defined as
the vector of feature-level attribution scores produced by an explainer

E
for an input
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Each component
E_{i}(x)
represents
the
contribution
of the input feature
x_{i}
to the model’s
prediction at
x
.
3
3

3

We use the term
attribution score
to
denote feature-level contribution throughout this paper.
This definition is intentionally general and encompasses a wide range of
attribution methods, including gradient-based methods
(Gradient
\times
Input, IG), perturbation-based methods
(Occlusion, SmoothGrad), and game-theoretic methods (SHAP).

Axiom 1

(
Stability Under Small Perturbations
)
.

Let
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and let
\delta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
satisfy
\|\delta\|\leq\epsilon
.
Assume that the explanation map
E:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is locally Lipschitz at
x
,
i.e., there exist
r>0
and a constant
C_{E}(x)>0
such that for all
u,v\in B(x,r)
,

d\!\left(E(u),E(v)\right)\leq C_{E}(x)\,\|u-v\|.
Then, for all perturbations
\delta
with
\|\delta\|\leq\min\{\epsilon,r\}
,

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle d\!\left(E(x),\,E(x+\delta)\right)\leq C_{E}(x)\,\|\delta\|\leq C_{E}(x)\,\epsilon.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle d\!\left(E(x),\,E(x+\delta)\right)\leq C_{E}(x)\,\|\delta\|\leq C_{E}(x)\,\epsilon.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle d\!\left(E(x),\,E(x+\delta)\right)\leq C_{E}(x)\,\|\delta\|\leq C_{E}(x)\,\epsilon.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle d\!\left(E(x),\,E(x+\delta)\right)\leq C_{E}(x)\,\|\delta\|\leq C_{E}(x)\,\epsilon.$}}}

Axiom 2

(
Redundancy-Collapse Consistency
)
.

Let
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle x=[x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{d}]\in\mathbb{R}^{d}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle x=[x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{d}]\in\mathbb{R}^{d}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle x=[x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{d}]\in\mathbb{R}^{d}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x=[x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{d}]\in\mathbb{R}^{d}$}}}
denote an input and

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle E(x)=[E_{1}(x),E_{2}(x),\ldots,E_{d}(x)]$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle E(x)=[E_{1}(x),E_{2}(x),\ldots,E_{d}(x)]$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle E(x)=[E_{1}(x),E_{2}(x),\ldots,E_{d}(x)]$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle E(x)=[E_{1}(x),E_{2}(x),\ldots,E_{d}(x)]$}}}
its feature-wise explanation.
Assume that feature
x_{j}
becomes asymptotically redundant with respect to
feature
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle x_{i}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle x_{i}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle x_{i}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x_{i}$}}}
according to the redundancy model,
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle x_{j}=\alpha x_{i}+\sqrt{1-\alpha^{2}}\,Z,$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle x_{j}=\alpha x_{i}+\sqrt{1-\alpha^{2}}\,Z,$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle x_{j}=\alpha x_{i}+\sqrt{1-\alpha^{2}}\,Z,$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x_{j}=\alpha x_{i}+\sqrt{1-\alpha^{2}}\,Z,$}}}

where
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle Z$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle Z$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle Z$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle Z$}}}
is independent noise and
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\alpha\in[0,1)$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\alpha\in[0,1)$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\alpha\in[0,1)$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\alpha\in[0,1)$}}}
controls the degree of
redundancy. Let’s
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle E^{\mathrm{col}}(x)$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle E^{\mathrm{col}}(x)$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle E^{\mathrm{col}}(x)$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle E^{\mathrm{col}}(x)$}}}
denote the explanation obtained after
collapsing the redundant feature pair
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle(i,j)$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle(i,j)$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle(i,j)$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle(i,j)$}}}
into a single effective feature.
Then a redundancy-consistent explanation method should satisfy,
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\lim_{\alpha\to 1}d\!\left(E^{\mathrm{col}}(x),\,E(x^{\mathrm{col}})\right)=0.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\lim_{\alpha\to 1}d\!\left(E^{\mathrm{col}}(x),\,E(x^{\mathrm{col}})\right)=0.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\lim_{\alpha\to 1}d\!\left(E^{\mathrm{col}}(x),\,E(x^{\mathrm{col}})\right)=0.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\lim_{\alpha\to 1}d\!\left(E^{\mathrm{col}}(x),\,E(x^{\mathrm{col}})\right)=0.$}}}

Definition 1

(
Collapse Operator.
)
.

To operationalize Axiom
2
, we define a deterministic
collapse operator

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle C_{i\leftarrow j}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d-1}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle C_{i\leftarrow j}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d-1}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle C_{i\leftarrow j}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d-1}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle C_{i\leftarrow j}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d-1}$}}}
for a redundant feature
pair
(i,j)
. The operator acts as (i) the feature
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle j$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle j$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle j$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle j$}}}
is removed from the input, and (ii) the feature
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle i$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle i$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle i$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle i$}}}
is compensated by absorbing the redundant component of
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle x_{j}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle x_{j}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle x_{j}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x_{j}$}}}
. For the synthetic redundancy model

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle x_{j}=\alpha x_{i}+\sqrt{1-\alpha^{2}}\,Z$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle x_{j}=\alpha x_{i}+\sqrt{1-\alpha^{2}}\,Z$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle x_{j}=\alpha x_{i}+\sqrt{1-\alpha^{2}}\,Z$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x_{j}=\alpha x_{i}+\sqrt{1-\alpha^{2}}\,Z$}}}
,
the collapsed input is defined as,
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle x^{\mathrm{col}}:=C_{i\leftarrow j}(x);\ x_{i}^{\mathrm{col}}=x_{i}+\alpha x_{i}.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle x^{\mathrm{col}}:=C_{i\leftarrow j}(x);\ x_{i}^{\mathrm{col}}=x_{i}+\alpha x_{i}.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle x^{\mathrm{col}}:=C_{i\leftarrow j}(x);\ x_{i}^{\mathrm{col}}=x_{i}+\alpha x_{i}.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x^{\mathrm{col}}:=C_{i\leftarrow j}(x);\ x_{i}^{\mathrm{col}}=x_{i}+\alpha x_{i}.$}}}

Definition 2

(
Collapsed Explanation
)
.

Let
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle P_{-j}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d-1}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle P_{-j}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d-1}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle P_{-j}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d-1}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle P_{-j}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d-1}$}}}
denote the projection operator
that removes the
j
-th coordinate. The collapsed explanation is defined as,

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle E^{\mathrm{col}}(x):=P_{-j}(E(x)).$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle E^{\mathrm{col}}(x):=P_{-j}(E(x)).$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle E^{\mathrm{col}}(x):=P_{-j}(E(x)).$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle E^{\mathrm{col}}(x):=P_{-j}(E(x)).$}}}\vskip-5.69054pt

(1)

When two features carry the same information, explanations
should converge rather than redistribute importance.

Axiom 3

(
Smooth Evolution
)
.

Let
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Delta(\cdot)$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Delta(\cdot)$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\Delta(\cdot)$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta(\cdot)$}}}
denote the explanation drift induced by a small, non-adversarial
transformation (e.g., input perturbation, redundancy injection, model update,
temporal or distributional shift). Explanation reliability is defined as

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathrm{ERI}(x)=\psi\!\big(\Delta(x)\big),$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathrm{ERI}(x)=\psi\!\big(\Delta(x)\big),$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathrm{ERI}(x)=\psi\!\big(\Delta(x)\big),$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{ERI}(x)=\psi\!\big(\Delta(x)\big),$}}}

where
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\psi:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to(0,1]$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\psi:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to(0,1]$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\psi:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to(0,1]$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\psi:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to(0,1]$}}}
satisfies the
properties,
Strict monotonicity:

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\psi$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\psi$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\psi$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\psi$}}}
is strictly decreasing in

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Delta$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Delta$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\Delta$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}}
.
Continuity at zero:

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\lim_{\Delta\to 0}\psi(\Delta)=1.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\lim_{\Delta\to 0}\psi(\Delta)=1.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\lim_{\Delta\to 0}\psi(\Delta)=1.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\lim_{\Delta\to 0}\psi(\Delta)=1.$}}}

Smooth evolution:
for any sequence of small, non-adversarial transformations
inducing
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Delta_{n}\to 0$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Delta_{n}\to 0$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\Delta_{n}\to 0$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta_{n}\to 0$}}}
(e.g.,

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\|\theta_{k+1}-\theta_{k}\|\to 0$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\|\theta_{k+1}-\theta_{k}\|\to 0$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\|\theta_{k+1}-\theta_{k}\|\to 0$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\|\theta_{k+1}-\theta_{k}\|\to 0$}}}
in model evolution), the
corresponding reliability scores satisfy

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathrm{ERI}_{n}=\psi(\Delta_{n})\to 1.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathrm{ERI}_{n}=\psi(\Delta_{n})\to 1.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathrm{ERI}_{n}=\psi(\Delta_{n})\to 1.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{ERI}_{n}=\psi(\Delta_{n})\to 1.$}}}

Axiom 4

(
Distributional Robustness
)
.

Let
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{P}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{P}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathcal{P}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{P}$}}}
and
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{\prime}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{\prime}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathcal{P}^{\prime}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{P}^{\prime}$}}}
be two probability
distributions on
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{d}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{d}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}^{d}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}^{d}$}}}
.
Let
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle d_{\mathcal{P}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle d_{\mathcal{P}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle d_{\mathcal{P}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle d_{\mathcal{P}}$}}}
be a probability metric that admits a
Kantorovich–Rubinstein dual representation over Lipschitz test functions
(e.g., the Wasserstein–
1
distance).
Assume that,
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle d_{\mathcal{P}}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{\prime})\leq\epsilon.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle d_{\mathcal{P}}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{\prime})\leq\epsilon.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle d_{\mathcal{P}}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{\prime})\leq\epsilon.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle d_{\mathcal{P}}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{\prime})\leq\epsilon.$}}}

Assume further that the explanation map

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle E:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle E:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle E:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle E:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}$}}}
is

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle L_{E}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle L_{E}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle L_{E}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle L_{E}$}}}
–Lipschitz with respect to the input norm

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\|\cdot\|$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\|\cdot\|$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\|\cdot\|$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\|\cdot\|$}}}
and the attribution-space dissimilarity

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle d(\cdot,\cdot)$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle d(\cdot,\cdot)$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle d(\cdot,\cdot)$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle d(\cdot,\cdot)$}}}
, i.e.,
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle d\!\left(E(x),E(x^{\prime})\right)\leq L_{E}\,\|x-x^{\prime}\|\qquad\forall\,x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle d\!\left(E(x),E(x^{\prime})\right)\leq L_{E}\,\|x-x^{\prime}\|\qquad\forall\,x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle d\!\left(E(x),E(x^{\prime})\right)\leq L_{E}\,\|x-x^{\prime}\|\qquad\forall\,x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle d\!\left(E(x),E(x^{\prime})\right)\leq L_{E}\,\|x-x^{\prime}\|\hskip 16.38895pt\forall\,x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.$}}}
Then a distributionally robust explanation method satisfies
4
4

4

The bound applies to probability metrics that capture smooth, gradual
distributional shifts (e.g., Wasserstein-
1
). It does not hold in general for
metrics such as total variation without extra boundedness assumptions on
E
.
,
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle d\!\left(\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathcal{P}}[E(x)],\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}[E(x)]\right)\leq L_{E}\,d_{\mathcal{P}}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{\prime})\leq L_{E}\,\epsilon.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle d\!\left(\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathcal{P}}[E(x)],\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}[E(x)]\right)\leq L_{E}\,d_{\mathcal{P}}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{\prime})\leq L_{E}\,\epsilon.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle d\!\left(\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathcal{P}}[E(x)],\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}[E(x)]\right)\leq L_{E}\,d_{\mathcal{P}}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{\prime})\leq L_{E}\,\epsilon.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle d\!\left(\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathcal{P}}[E(x)],\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}[E(x)]\right)\leq L_{E}\,d_{\mathcal{P}}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{\prime})\leq L_{E}\,\epsilon.$}}}

4
The Explanation Reliability Index (ERI)

Recall that
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
denotes an input and
E(x)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
its
corresponding explanation vector, where
E_{i}(x)
represents the attribution of
feature
x_{i}
to the model’s prediction. We assess explanation reliability under
small, non-adversarial, non-adversarial
transformations
that reflect realistic operating conditions (e.g., noise,
redundancy, temporal or distributional drift), rather than worst-case
perturbations. Accordingly, stability is measured in expectation over a transformation
distribution, capturing typical behavior rather than adversarial robustness.

Definition 3

(
Explanation Drift
)
.

Let
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle E:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle E:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle E:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle E:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}$}}}
denote an explanation method and let

\mathcal{T}
be a family of
small, non-adversarial transformations
5
5

5

Each transformation
\tau\in\mathcal{T}
may act on
(i) the input space (
x\mapsto\tau(x)
),
(ii) the feature structure (e.g., redundancy or collapse),
(iii) the model parameters (
\theta\mapsto\tau(\theta)
), or
(iv) the data-generating process (distributional or temporal shift).
acting on the
explanation pipeline. Let
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\tau_{\omega}\sim\Omega$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\tau_{\omega}\sim\Omega$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\tau_{\omega}\sim\Omega$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\tau_{\omega}\sim\Omega$}}}
denote a random transformation drawn from a
distribution
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Omega$ over $\mathcal{T}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Omega$ over $\mathcal{T}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\Omega$ over $\mathcal{T}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Omega$ over $\mathcal{T}$}}}
.
The explanation drift is defined as

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Delta(x):=\mathbb{E}_{\omega\sim\Omega}\big[d\big(E(x),\,E(\tau_{\omega}(x))\big)\big],$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Delta(x):=\mathbb{E}_{\omega\sim\Omega}\big[d\big(E(x),\,E(\tau_{\omega}(x))\big)\big],$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\Delta(x):=\mathbb{E}_{\omega\sim\Omega}\big[d\big(E(x),\,E(\tau_{\omega}(x))\big)\big],$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta(x):=\mathbb{E}_{\omega\sim\Omega}\big[d\big(E(x),\,E(\tau_{\omega}(x))\big)\big],$}}}
where
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle d:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle d:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle d:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle d:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}$}}}
is a non-negative
dissimilarity function.

Definition 4

(
Explanation Reliability Index (ERI)
6
6

6

High ERI values indicate stable explanations, while low ERI values indicate
noise-like or unreliable behavior. This transformation ensures boundedness
and preserves the ordering induced by drift.
.)
.

The Explanation The reliability index is defined as a bounded, monotone
transformation of the drift:

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathrm{ERI}(x):=\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+\Delta(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+\Delta(x)}}\in(0,1].$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathrm{ERI}(x):=\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+\Delta(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+\Delta(x)}}\in(0,1].$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathrm{ERI}(x):=\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+\Delta(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+\Delta(x)}}\in(0,1].$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{ERI}(x):=\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+\Delta(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+\Delta(x)}}\in(0,1].$}}}\vskip-11.38109pt

(2)

A value of
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathrm{ERI}(x)$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathrm{ERI}(x)$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathrm{ERI}(x)$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{ERI}(x)$}}}
close to
1
indicates a highly stable explanation,
whereas values near
0
reflect instability or noise-like behavior.
Geometrically,
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle E(x)$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle E(x)$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle E(x)$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle E(x)$}}}
can be viewed as a point in attribution space, while small, non-adversarial
transformations generate a cloud of transformed explanations

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\{E(\tau_{\omega}(x))\}_{\omega\sim\Omega}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\{E(\tau_{\omega}(x))\}_{\omega\sim\Omega}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\{E(\tau_{\omega}(x))\}_{\omega\sim\Omega}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\{E(\tau_{\omega}(x))\}_{\omega\sim\Omega}$}}}
around it (Figure
7
).
The drift
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Delta(x)$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Delta(x)$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\Delta(x)$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta(x)$}}}
measures the
expected
deviation of this cloud from

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle E(x)$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle E(x)$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle E(x)$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle E(x)$}}}
under typical operating variability, and ERI applies the bounded monotone
map
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle u\mapsto(1+u)^{-1}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle u\mapsto(1+u)^{-1}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle u\mapsto(1+u)^{-1}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle u\mapsto(1+u)^{-1}$}}}
so that smaller drift corresponds to higher reliability. Different ERI variants correspond to different choices of the small, non-adversarial
transformation family
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{T}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{T}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathcal{T}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{T}$}}}
and sampling distribution

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Omega$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Omega$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\Omega$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Omega$}}}
.
Specifically, ERI-S uses

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\tau_{\omega}(x)=x+\delta$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\tau_{\omega}(x)=x+\delta$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\tau_{\omega}(x)=x+\delta$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\tau_{\omega}(x)=x+\delta$}}},
where
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\delta$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\delta$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\delta$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\delta$}}}
denotes small, non-adversarial noise (e.g., bounded

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\ell_{p}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\ell_{p}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\ell_{p}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\ell_{p}$}}}
perturbations or Gaussian noise with controlled magnitude);
ERI-R applies transformations

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\tau_{\omega}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\tau_{\omega}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\tau_{\omega}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\tau_{\omega}$}}}
that inject feature redundancy or perform feature
collapse under an
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\alpha$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\alpha$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\alpha$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\alpha$}}}
-redundancy model; ERI-M
uses transformations

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\theta\mapsto\theta^{\prime}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\theta\mapsto\theta^{\prime}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\theta\mapsto\theta^{\prime}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\theta\mapsto\theta^{\prime}$}}},

corresponding to successive model checkpoints or retraining seeds, and compares
explanations
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle E_{\theta}(x)$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle E_{\theta}(x)$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle E_{\theta}(x)$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle E_{\theta}(x)$}}}
and
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle E_{\theta^{\prime}}(x)$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle E_{\theta^{\prime}}(x)$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle E_{\theta^{\prime}}(x)$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle E_{\theta^{\prime}}(x)$}}}
; ERI-D
evaluates small, non-adversarial shifts in the
data-generating process by comparing explanations under

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{P}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{P}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathcal{P}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{P}$}}}
and
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{\prime}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{\prime}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathcal{P}^{\prime}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{P}^{\prime}$}}}
; and ERI-T
induces temporal shifts along a sequence,
comparing explanations across adjacent time steps. The proposed evaluation framework consists of the
ERI family
, where each
variant scores reliability with respect to a specific stability property.
These components can optionally be aggregated via a fixed aggregation function

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Phi$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Phi$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\Phi$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Phi$}}}
7
7

7

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Phi$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Phi$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\Phi$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Phi$}}}
maps the ERI component scores to a single scalar, enabling direct comparison across explainers.
.
We evaluate explanation reliability using
ERI-Bench
8
8

8

Formal definitions of ERI aggregation and
ERI-Bench
are in the appendix Appendix

refagg, refbn. All related and additional theoretical results, including lemmas, propositions, and proofs, are provided in Appendices
B
-
F
,
I
-
O
.
, a standardized benchmarking protocol that systematically instantiates each ERI variant under controlled, non-adversarial transformations.

Definition 5

(
ERI-S: Perturbation Stability
)
.

Let
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\delta\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}I)$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\delta\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}I)$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\delta\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}I)$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\delta\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}I)$}}}
be a small, small, non-adversarial perturbation
with controlled scale
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\sigma$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\sigma$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\sigma$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sigma$}}}
, such that

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|\delta\|]\leq\epsilon$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|\delta\|]\leq\epsilon$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathbb{E}[\|\delta\|]\leq\epsilon$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{E}[\|\delta\|]\leq\epsilon$}}}
(or equivalently,

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\|\delta\|\leq\epsilon$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\|\delta\|\leq\epsilon$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\|\delta\|\leq\epsilon$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\|\delta\|\leq\epsilon$}}}
with high probability).
Define the perturbation-induced drift as,
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Delta_{S}(x):=\mathbb{E}_{\delta\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}I)}\!\left[d\big(E(x),\,E(x+\delta)\big)\right].$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Delta_{S}(x):=\mathbb{E}_{\delta\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}I)}\!\left[d\big(E(x),\,E(x+\delta)\big)\right].$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\Delta_{S}(x):=\mathbb{E}_{\delta\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}I)}\!\left[d\big(E(x),\,E(x+\delta)\big)\right].$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta_{S}(x):=\mathbb{E}_{\delta\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}I)}\!\left[d\big(E(x),\,E(x+\delta)\big)\right].$}}}

The perturbation-stability component of ERI is then,

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathrm{ERI\text{-}S}(x)\;=\;\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_S(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_S(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+\Delta_S(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+\Delta_S(x)}}.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathrm{ERI\text{-}S}(x)\;=\;\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_S(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_S(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+\Delta_S(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+\Delta_S(x)}}.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathrm{ERI\text{-}S}(x)\;=\;\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_S(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_S(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+\Delta_S(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+\Delta_S(x)}}.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{ERI\text{-}S}(x)\;=\;\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_S(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_S(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+\Delta_S(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+\Delta_S(x)}}.$}}}\vskip-5.69054pt

(3)

ERI-S quantifies how smoothly the explanation changes under small, small, non-adversarial input
variations.

Definition 6

(
Redundancy Drift (ERI-R)
)
.

The redundancy-induced drift at redundancy level
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\alpha$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\alpha$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\alpha$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\alpha$}}}
is defined as
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Delta_{R}(x;\alpha):=\mathbb{E}\!\left[d\!\left(E^{\mathrm{col}}(x),\,E(x^{\mathrm{col}})\right)\right].$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Delta_{R}(x;\alpha):=\mathbb{E}\!\left[d\!\left(E^{\mathrm{col}}(x),\,E(x^{\mathrm{col}})\right)\right].$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\Delta_{R}(x;\alpha):=\mathbb{E}\!\left[d\!\left(E^{\mathrm{col}}(x),\,E(x^{\mathrm{col}})\right)\right].$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta_{R}(x;\alpha):=\mathbb{E}\!\left[d\!\left(E^{\mathrm{col}}(x),\,E(x^{\mathrm{col}})\right)\right].$}}}
We define the overall redundancy drift by averaging across redundancy levels:
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Delta_{R}(x):=\mathbb{E}_{\alpha\sim\mathrm{Unif}[\alpha_{0},1)}\!\left[\Delta_{R}(x;\alpha)\right].$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Delta_{R}(x):=\mathbb{E}_{\alpha\sim\mathrm{Unif}[\alpha_{0},1)}\!\left[\Delta_{R}(x;\alpha)\right].$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\Delta_{R}(x):=\mathbb{E}_{\alpha\sim\mathrm{Unif}[\alpha_{0},1)}\!\left[\Delta_{R}(x;\alpha)\right].$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta_{R}(x):=\mathbb{E}_{\alpha\sim\mathrm{Unif}[\alpha_{0},1)}\!\left[\Delta_{R}(x;\alpha)\right].$}}}
The redundancy reliability index is then given by,

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathrm{ERI\text{-}R}(x):=\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathrm{ERI\text{-}R}(x):=\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathrm{ERI\text{-}R}(x):=\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{ERI\text{-}R}(x):=\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}.$}}}\vskip-5.69054pt

(4)

Definition 7

(
ERI-M
9
9

9

The single-step definition corresponds to the special case of the
trajectory-based ERI-M with
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle K=2$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle K=2$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle K=2$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle K=2$}}}
. In practice, we report the
trajectory-averaged ERI-M unless stated otherwise.
: Model-Evolution Consistency
)
.

Let
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle E_{t}(x)$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle E_{t}(x)$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle E_{t}(x)$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle E_{t}(x)$}}}
and
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle E_{t+\Delta}(x)$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle E_{t+\Delta}(x)$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle E_{t+\Delta}(x)$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle E_{t+\Delta}(x)$}}}
denote explanations generated by model
parameters
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\theta_{t}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\theta_{t}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\theta_{t}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\theta_{t}$}}}
and
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\theta_{t+\Delta}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\theta_{t+\Delta}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\theta_{t+\Delta}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\theta_{t+\Delta}$}}}
, respectively, where

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\|\theta_{t+\Delta}-\theta_{t}\|\leq\Delta$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\|\theta_{t+\Delta}-\theta_{t}\|\leq\Delta$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\|\theta_{t+\Delta}-\theta_{t}\|\leq\Delta$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\|\theta_{t+\Delta}-\theta_{t}\|\leq\Delta$}}}
corresponds to successive training
checkpoints or retraining with different random seeds. Define the
model-evolution drift as,
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Delta_{M}(x):=d\big(E_{t}(x),\,E_{t+\Delta}(x)\big).$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Delta_{M}(x):=d\big(E_{t}(x),\,E_{t+\Delta}(x)\big).$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\Delta_{M}(x):=d\big(E_{t}(x),\,E_{t+\Delta}(x)\big).$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta_{M}(x):=d\big(E_{t}(x),\,E_{t+\Delta}(x)\big).$}}}
The model-evolution component of ERI is then given by

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathrm{ERI\text{-}M}(x)\;=\;\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_M(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_M(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+\Delta_M(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+\Delta_M(x)}}.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathrm{ERI\text{-}M}(x)\;=\;\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_M(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_M(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+\Delta_M(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+\Delta_M(x)}}.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathrm{ERI\text{-}M}(x)\;=\;\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_M(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_M(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+\Delta_M(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+\Delta_M(x)}}.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{ERI\text{-}M}(x)\;=\;\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_M(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_M(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+\Delta_M(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+\Delta_M(x)}}.$}}}

(5)

Definition 8

(
ERI-D: Distributional Robustness
)
.

Let
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
be input distributions differing by a small,
non-adversarial shift. Define the distributional drift as
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Delta_{D}:=d\!\left(\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathcal{P}}[E(x)],\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}[E(x)]\right).$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Delta_{D}:=d\!\left(\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathcal{P}}[E(x)],\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}[E(x)]\right).$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\Delta_{D}:=d\!\left(\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathcal{P}}[E(x)],\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}[E(x)]\right).$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta_{D}:=d\!\left(\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathcal{P}}[E(x)],\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}[E(x)]\right).$}}}
The distributional reliability score is,

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathrm{ERI\text{-}D}=\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_D}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_D}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+\Delta_D}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+\Delta_D}}.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathrm{ERI\text{-}D}=\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_D}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_D}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+\Delta_D}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+\Delta_D}}.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathrm{ERI\text{-}D}=\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_D}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_D}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+\Delta_D}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+\Delta_D}}.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{ERI\text{-}D}=\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_D}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_D}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+\Delta_D}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+\Delta_D}}.$}}}\vskip-5.69054pt

(6)

Definition 9

(
ERI-T: Temporal Reliability
)
.

Let
(x_{t})_{t=1}^{T}
be a temporal sequence and
E(x_{t})
its corresponding
explanations. The temporal component of ERI is defined as,

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathrm{ERI\text{-}T}=\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\frac{1}{T-1}
\sum_{t=1}^{T-1}
d \big(E(x_t),\,E(x_{t+1})\big)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\frac{1}{T-1}
\sum_{t=1}^{T-1}
d \big(E(x_t),\,E(x_{t+1})\big)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+\frac{1}{T-1}
\sum_{t=1}^{T-1}
d \big(E(x_t),\,E(x_{t+1})\big)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+\frac{1}{T-1}
\sum_{t=1}^{T-1}
d \big(E(x_t),\,E(x_{t+1})\big)}}.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathrm{ERI\text{-}T}=\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\frac{1}{T-1}
\sum_{t=1}^{T-1}
d \big(E(x_t),\,E(x_{t+1})\big)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\frac{1}{T-1}
\sum_{t=1}^{T-1}
d \big(E(x_t),\,E(x_{t+1})\big)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+\frac{1}{T-1}
\sum_{t=1}^{T-1}
d \big(E(x_t),\,E(x_{t+1})\big)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+\frac{1}{T-1}
\sum_{t=1}^{T-1}
d \big(E(x_t),\,E(x_{t+1})\big)}}.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathrm{ERI\text{-}T}=\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\frac{1}{T-1}
\sum_{t=1}^{T-1}
d \big(E(x_t),\,E(x_{t+1})\big)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\frac{1}{T-1}
\sum_{t=1}^{T-1}
d \big(E(x_t),\,E(x_{t+1})\big)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+\frac{1}{T-1}
\sum_{t=1}^{T-1}
d \big(E(x_t),\,E(x_{t+1})\big)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+\frac{1}{T-1}
\sum_{t=1}^{T-1}
d \big(E(x_t),\,E(x_{t+1})\big)}}.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{ERI\text{-}T}=\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\frac{1}{T-1}
\sum_{t=1}^{T-1}
d \big(E(x_t),\,E(x_{t+1})\big)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\frac{1}{T-1}
\sum_{t=1}^{T-1}
d \big(E(x_t),\,E(x_{t+1})\big)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+\frac{1}{T-1}
\sum_{t=1}^{T-1}
d \big(E(x_t),\,E(x_{t+1})\big)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+\frac{1}{T-1}
\sum_{t=1}^{T-1}
d \big(E(x_t),\,E(x_{t+1})\big)}}.$}}}\vskip-5.69054pt

(7)

ERI is not only a diagnostic measure but a decision-altering signal, enabling reliability-aware model or checkpoint selection among comparably accurate candidates.

5
Theoretical Guarantees

We present theoretical guarantees for ERI and its variants, analyzing how explanation reliability responds to input perturbations, feature redundancy, and temporal evolution.
10
10

10

Let
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle f_{\theta}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle f_{\theta}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle f_{\theta}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle f_{\theta}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}$}}}
denote the predictive model and
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle E:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle E:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle E:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle E:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}$}}}
the associated explanation map. We assume standard Lipschitz continuity of both
f_{\theta}
and
E
with respect to their arguments.

Theorem 1

(
Lipschitz Stability Bound
)
.

Assume the predictive model
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}$}}}
is locally

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle L_{f}(x)$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle L_{f}(x)$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle L_{f}(x)$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle L_{f}(x)$}}}
-Lipschitz in a neighborhood of
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle x$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle x$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle x$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}}}
, i.e.,

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\|f(x)-f(x+\delta)\|\leq L_{f}(x)\,\|\delta\|$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\|f(x)-f(x+\delta)\|\leq L_{f}(x)\,\|\delta\|$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\|f(x)-f(x+\delta)\|\leq L_{f}(x)\,\|\delta\|$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\|f(x)-f(x+\delta)\|\leq L_{f}(x)\,\|\delta\|$}}}\ \text{for all }\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\|\delta\|\leq\epsilon$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\|\delta\|\leq\epsilon$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\|\delta\|\leq\epsilon$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\|\delta\|\leq\epsilon$}}},
and the explanation map
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle E:\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle E:\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle E:\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle E:\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}$}}}
is

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle L_{E}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle L_{E}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle L_{E}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle L_{E}$}}}
-Lipschitz with respect to its input.
Then the expected explanation drift under perturbations satisfies,
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Delta_{S}(x):=\mathbb{E}_{\delta}\!\left[d\!\big(E(f(x)),E(f(x+\delta))\big)\right]\;\leq\;L_{E}\,L_{f}(x)\,\epsilon.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Delta_{S}(x):=\mathbb{E}_{\delta}\!\left[d\!\big(E(f(x)),E(f(x+\delta))\big)\right]\;\leq\;L_{E}\,L_{f}(x)\,\epsilon.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\Delta_{S}(x):=\mathbb{E}_{\delta}\!\left[d\!\big(E(f(x)),E(f(x+\delta))\big)\right]\;\leq\;L_{E}\,L_{f}(x)\,\epsilon.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta_{S}(x):=\mathbb{E}_{\delta}\!\left[d\!\big(E(f(x)),E(f(x+\delta))\big)\right]\;\leq\;L_{E}\,L_{f}(x)\,\epsilon.$}}}

Consequently, the perturbation-stability ERI component, defined as

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathrm{ERI\mbox{-}S}(x)=\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_S(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_S(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+\Delta_S(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+\Delta_S(x)}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathrm{ERI\mbox{-}S}(x)=\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_S(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_S(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+\Delta_S(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+\Delta_S(x)}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathrm{ERI\mbox{-}S}(x)=\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_S(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_S(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+\Delta_S(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+\Delta_S(x)}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{ERI\mbox{-}S}(x)=\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_S(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_S(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+\Delta_S(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+\Delta_S(x)}}$}}}
, obeys

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathrm{ERI\mbox{-}S}(x)\;\geq\;\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+L_E\,L_f(x)\,\epsilon}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+L_E\,L_f(x)\,\epsilon}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+L_E\,L_f(x)\,\epsilon}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+L_E\,L_f(x)\,\epsilon}}.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathrm{ERI\mbox{-}S}(x)\;\geq\;\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+L_E\,L_f(x)\,\epsilon}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+L_E\,L_f(x)\,\epsilon}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+L_E\,L_f(x)\,\epsilon}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+L_E\,L_f(x)\,\epsilon}}.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathrm{ERI\mbox{-}S}(x)\;\geq\;\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+L_E\,L_f(x)\,\epsilon}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+L_E\,L_f(x)\,\epsilon}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+L_E\,L_f(x)\,\epsilon}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+L_E\,L_f(x)\,\epsilon}}.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{ERI\mbox{-}S}(x)\;\geq\;\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+L_E\,L_f(x)\,\epsilon}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+L_E\,L_f(x)\,\epsilon}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+L_E\,L_f(x)\,\epsilon}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+L_E\,L_f(x)\,\epsilon}}.$}}}

Theorem 2

(
Redundancy-Collapse Convergence
11
11

11

As features become perfectly redundant (
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\alpha\to 1$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\alpha\to 1$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\alpha\to 1$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\alpha\to 1$}}}
), their attributions should converge, a property captured by ERI-R but often violated by SHAP, IG, and SAGE, while sequential inputs
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle x_{1},\ldots,x_{T}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle x_{1},\ldots,x_{T}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle x_{1},\ldots,x_{T}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x_{1},\ldots,x_{T}$}}}
evolve over time.
)
.

Consider the redundancy model,
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle x_{j}=\alpha x_{i}+\sqrt{1-\alpha^{2}}\,Z,$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle x_{j}=\alpha x_{i}+\sqrt{1-\alpha^{2}}\,Z,$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle x_{j}=\alpha x_{i}+\sqrt{1-\alpha^{2}}\,Z,$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x_{j}=\alpha x_{i}+\sqrt{1-\alpha^{2}}\,Z,$}}}

where
Z
is zero-mean noise independent of
x_{i}
and
\alpha\in[0,1)
.
Assume the explainer
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle E:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle E:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle E:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle E:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}$}}}
satisfies mild
regularity conditions.
12
12

12

Specifically, we assume that
(i)
E
is continuous with respect to its input and
(ii)
E
is consistent with the redundancy collapse operator, i.e.,
letting
x^{\mathrm{col}}
denote the input obtained by collapsing the redundant
feature pair
(i,j)
,

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\lim_{\alpha\to 1}d\!\big(E(x),E(x^{\mathrm{col}})\big)=0.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\lim_{\alpha\to 1}d\!\big(E(x),E(x^{\mathrm{col}})\big)=0.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\lim_{\alpha\to 1}d\!\big(E(x),E(x^{\mathrm{col}})\big)=0.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\lim_{\alpha\to 1}d\!\big(E(x),E(x^{\mathrm{col}})\big)=0.$}}}

Then the redundancy-based reliability score satisfies

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\lim_{\alpha\to 1}\mathrm{ERI\text{-}R}(x)=1.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\lim_{\alpha\to 1}\mathrm{ERI\text{-}R}(x)=1.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\lim_{\alpha\to 1}\mathrm{ERI\text{-}R}(x)=1.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\lim_{\alpha\to 1}\mathrm{ERI\text{-}R}(x)=1.$}}}

Definition 10

(
Temporal Drift
13
13

13

In time-dependent systems (e.g., energy demand or temperature forecasting), smoothly evolving inputs should yield smoothly varying explanations within a stable operating regime, while abrupt changes in explanations are expected only during regime transitions or genuine structural changes in the underlying signal.
).

Given an attribution trajectory
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\{h_{t}\}_{t=1}^{T}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\{h_{t}\}_{t=1}^{T}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\{h_{t}\}_{t=1}^{T}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\{h_{t}\}_{t=1}^{T}$}}}
produced across training
epochs, the
temporal drift
between consecutive epochs is defined as,
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Delta_{t}\;=\;\bigl\|\,h_{t+1}-h_{t}\,\bigr\|,$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Delta_{t}\;=\;\bigl\|\,h_{t+1}-h_{t}\,\bigr\|,$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\Delta_{t}\;=\;\bigl\|\,h_{t+1}-h_{t}\,\bigr\|,$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta_{t}\;=\;\bigl\|\,h_{t+1}-h_{t}\,\bigr\|,$}}}
where
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\|\cdot\|$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\|\cdot\|$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\|\cdot\|$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\|\cdot\|$}}}
denotes a chosen distance metric (e.g.,
\ell_{2}
,
cosine or Wasserstein). Larger values
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Delta_{t}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Delta_{t}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\Delta_{t}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta_{t}$}}}
indicate greater
instability of the explainer along the training trajectory.

Theorem 3

(
Temporal Stability Bound for ERI-T
)
.

If the explanation map
E
is
L_{E}
-Lipschitz with respect to the hidden state,
i.e.,

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle d\!\left(E(x_{t}),E(x_{t+1})\right)\leq L_{E}\,\Delta_{t},$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle d\!\left(E(x_{t}),E(x_{t+1})\right)\leq L_{E}\,\Delta_{t},$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle d\!\left(E(x_{t}),E(x_{t+1})\right)\leq L_{E}\,\Delta_{t},$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle d\!\left(E(x_{t}),E(x_{t+1})\right)\leq L_{E}\,\Delta_{t},$}}}

then the temporal reliability score satisfies,
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathrm{ERI\text{-}T}\;\geq\;\left(1+\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{L_E}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{L_E}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{L_E}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{L_E}{T-1}}\mathop{\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sum$}}}}\displaylimits_{t=1}^{T-1}\Delta_{t}\right)^{-1}.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathrm{ERI\text{-}T}\;\geq\;\left(1+\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{L_E}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{L_E}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{L_E}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{L_E}{T-1}}\mathop{\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sum$}}}}\displaylimits_{t=1}^{T-1}\Delta_{t}\right)^{-1}.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathrm{ERI\text{-}T}\;\geq\;\left(1+\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{L_E}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{L_E}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{L_E}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{L_E}{T-1}}\mathop{\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sum$}}}}\displaylimits_{t=1}^{T-1}\Delta_{t}\right)^{-1}.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{ERI\text{-}T}\;\geq\;\left(1+\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{L_E}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{L_E}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{L_E}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{L_E}{T-1}}\mathop{\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sum$}}}}\displaylimits_{t=1}^{T-1}\Delta_{t}\right)^{-1}.$}}}

Computational Complexity:

All ERI variants are computed
post hoc
and introduce no additional cost during model training.
The computational overhead scales linearly ith the number of explanation evaluations required:
ERI-S scales with the number of perturbations,
ERI-T with the sequence length,
and ERI-M with the number of model checkpoints.
Since ERI reuses attribution computations produced by the underlying explainer,
it does not alter the explainer’s asymptotic complexity and remains proportional
to the chosen evaluation budget.
This property is critical for applying reliability analysis repeatedly across datasets,
training checkpoints, and long temporal sequences.
Appendix
M
provides additional empirical results and detailed analyses
demonstrating that ERI remains computationally lightweight in large-scale and time-dependent
settings. Absolute wall-clock runtimes and a detailed analysis of ERI’s computational overhead
are reported in Appendix
Table 9
.

6
Experiments

6.1
Datasets, Explainers & ERI Metrics.

In this section, we evaluate ERI-Bench across four datasets: EEG microstates

(
24
;
17
)
,
UCI HAR activity recognition
(
3
)
,
Norwegian electricity load forecasting (NO1–NO5)

(
25
;
11
)
,
and CIFAR-10
(
20
)
. The energy forecasting dataset consists of long-horizon, multivariate hourly load time series spanning multiple years across five zones (NO1–NO5), resulting in many temporal observations with moderately correlated features. We benchmark IG, SHAP (DeepSHAP), DeepLIFT, Permutation Importance, SAGE, MCIR, MI, HSIC, and a random baseline. ERI-S, ERI-R, ERI-T, and ERI-M are computed using Gaussian input noise, synthetic feature redundancy, temporal smoothness, and checkpoint drift, respectively, with 10 random seeds and 500 Monte Carlo samples. We report drift values
\Delta
, related to reliability by Eq.
2
, where larger
\Delta
indicates lower reliability. For EEG microstates and UCI HAR, we use two-layer MLPs (ReLU, width 128) trained with Adam and early stopping; for Norwegian load forecasting, a two-layer LSTM (hidden size 64) with a linear readout; and for CIFAR-10, a ResNet-18. All models use standard dataset splits and hyperparameters, with results averaged over 10 random initializations. IG and DeepLIFT use a zero-input baseline (dataset-normalized), SHAP uses DeepSHAP with 100 background samples, and permutation importance uses 10 shuffles per feature. All explainers are evaluated on the same trained models without retraining. Although MI and HSIC are global dependence measures, we obtain instance-specific attribution vectors by estimating feature-wise conditional dependence in a local neighborhood of each input, using Gaussian perturbations centered at the instance; these local dependence vectors are treated as explanations and evaluated using the same ERI components as other methods.

6.2
Results and Discussion

This section evaluates ERI-S, ERI-R, ERI-T, and ERI-M across eight explanation methods and four domains, with quantitative results reported in Table
1
14
14

14

Note:
For MCIR, the drift is identically zero by construction (corresponding to
\mathrm{ERI}=1
). For MI and HSIC, reported values correspond
to normalized dependence scores used as reference baselines rather than empirical
drift estimates.
and qualitative patterns illustrated in the accompanying figures.
Across datasets and reliability axes, three consistent regimes emerge.
First, trivially invariant methods (MI, HSIC) achieve near-maximal ERI scores by construction, yet provide little or no downstream utility.
Second, widely used local explainers (IG, SHAP, DeepLIFT) often achieve high predictive usefulness but exhibit pronounced reliability failures under redundancy, temporal evolution, or model updates.
Third, dependence-aware methods (MCIR) combine non-trivial input- and model-dependent behavior with strong reliability across all ERI dimensions
15
15

15

Since ERI is normalized to
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle(0,1]$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle(0,1]$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle(0,1]$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle(0,1]$}}}
, perfect reliability corresponds to
\mathrm{ERI}=1
, and empirical comparisons focus on which methods attain or approach this maximum under realistic variations.
.
This regime structure is stable across domains and explains the systematic patterns observed in Table
1
and the accompanying figures.

6.3
Representative Reliability Visualizations

In this section, we discuss various visualizations that help us understand how reliable different explainer methods are when interpreting machine learning models, particularly in terms of redundancy and temporal attributes. Overall, these visualizations highlight the value of ERI-R and ERI-T for assessing explainer reliability, with random baselines exhibiting extreme instability
16
16

Synthetic ERI-R Collapse Curves.

Figure
1
reports ERI-R behavior under controlled
redundancy using
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle x_{2}=\alpha x_{1}+\sqrt{1-\alpha^{2}}Z$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle x_{2}=\alpha x_{1}+\sqrt{1-\alpha^{2}}Z$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle x_{2}=\alpha x_{1}+\sqrt{1-\alpha^{2}}Z$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x_{2}=\alpha x_{1}+\sqrt{1-\alpha^{2}}Z$}}}
.
The linear curve
1-\alpha
is shown as a
reference baseline
indicating
the degree of redundancy
17
17

17

This curve is included for interpretive reference
only, not a theoretical requirement of ERI-R.
.
MCIR exhibits a strictly stronger behavior: its redundancy drift remains
approximately zero across all
\alpha
, reflecting explicit redundancy-aware
collapse. In contrast, MI and HSIC inflate as
\alpha\to 1
, while SHAP, IG, and DeepLIFT
deviate substantially from the reference trend, exhibiting instability under
increasing feature dependence.
These results demonstrate that ERI–R quantitatively captures redundancy
robustness beyond visual inspection.

2.

Temporal Attribution in EEG Sequences.

Figure
3
shows temporal attribution paths for EEG microstate sequences.
Integrated Gradients achieves high temporal coherence (ERI–T
=0.9769
), with smooth transitions aligned to microstate boundaries,
whereas SHAP and DeepLIFT produce noisier and less consistent trajectories.
This confirms that ERI-T measures alignment with intrinsic temporal structure rather than prediction smoothness alone.

CIFAR–10 reliability diagnostics for IG: (a) perturbation robustness under bounded noise, (b) attribution map illustrating gradient saturation, and (c) deletion-curve instability.

CIFAR-10: IG Reliability Under CNN:

This experiment examines how reliable IG are when used with a ResNet-18 classifier on the CIFAR-10 dataset. The results show that IG is stable, with high ERI scores: ERI-S = 0.9921, ERI-R = 0.8117, and ERI-M = 0.9868. This means that IG’s attributions do not change much even when noise is added or when different training checkpoints are used. However, the moderately low ERI-R score indicates that IG can be influenced by redundant spatial patterns in the data. In Figure
2(a)
, the robustness histogram clusters around zero with a median of 0.0020, reaffirming that small amounts of Gaussian noise barely impact IG maps. Looking at the attribution visualizations in Figure
2(b)
show that IG mainly highlights edges. Finally, deletion curve in Figure
2(c)
, we see non-monotonic behavior, which suggests a problem where IG focuses too much on edges and textures instead of more meaningful features. Although IG is reliable, it may not always be semantically accurate, emphasizing the importance of distinguishing between reliability and faithfulness in the context of explaining CNNs. As shown in Table
2
,
MCIR correctly merges the duplicate feature, whereas IG and DeepLIFT split attribution across both dimensions and MI/HSIC inflate their scores, mirroring synthetic collapse behavior and confirming ERI-R’s relevance in naturally correlated settings. Unlike existing tools that offer only intuitive plots, ERI enables a principled
