0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}}$}}}}{\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}}}{\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}}}\Big(v(\{i,j\})-v(\{i\})\Big).

(97)

Here
v(S)
denotes the value function induced by DeepSHAP, which computes
expectations relative to a fixed
background (reference) distribution
.

Step 4 (DeepSHAP value function and reference expectation).

In DeepSHAP (DeepLIFT-based SHAP), missing features are replaced by samples from
a background dataset and expectations are taken with respect to this reference
distribution:

v(S)=\mathbb{E}_{X_{\bar{S}}}\Big[f\big(x_{S},X_{\bar{S}}\big)\Big].

(98)

Under (
93
), this yields

v(\{i\})=v\,\mathbb{E}[X_{j}],\qquad v(\{j\})=x_{j}\,\mathbb{E}[X_{i}],

(99)

and

v(\{i,j\})=f(v,x_{j})=vx_{j},\qquad v(\emptyset)=\mathbb{E}[X_{i}X_{j}].

(100)

Substituting (
99
)–(
100
) into
(
96
)–(
97
) yields

\phi_{i}(x)=\mathchoice{\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}}$}}}}{\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}}$}}}}{\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}}}{\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}}}\Big(v\,\mathbb{E}[X_{j}]-\mathbb{E}[X_{i}X_{j}]\Big)+\mathchoice{\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}}$}}}}{\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}}$}}}}{\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}}}{\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}}}\Big(vx_{j}-x_{j}\,\mathbb{E}[X_{i}]\Big),

(101)

\phi_{j}(x)=\mathchoice{\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}}$}}}}{\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}}$}}}}{\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}}}{\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}}}\Big(x_{j}\,\mathbb{E}[X_{i}]-\mathbb{E}[X_{i}X_{j}]\Big)+\mathchoice{\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}}$}}}}{\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}}$}}}}{\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}}}{\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{2}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}}}\Big(vx_{j}-v\,\mathbb{E}[X_{j}]\Big).

(102)

Step 5 (Asymmetry persists under full redundancy).

Subtracting (
102
) from (
101
) gives

\phi_{i}(x)-\phi_{j}(x)=v\,\mathbb{E}[X_{j}]-x_{j}\,\mathbb{E}[X_{i}].

(103)

As
\alpha\to 1
,
x_{j}\to v
in probability, so

\lim_{\alpha\to 1}\big(\phi_{i}(x)-\phi_{j}(x)\big)=v\big(\mathbb{E}[X_{j}]-\mathbb{E}[X_{i}]\big),

(104)

which is non-zero whenever the background distribution has unequal feature
means.
Therefore,

\lim_{\alpha\to 1}\big|\phi_{i}(x)-\phi_{j}(x)\big|>0.

(105)

Step 6 (Generality of the result).

The asymmetry arises whenever the DeepSHAP background distribution is not
feature-symmetric, which is common in practice due to feature scaling,
heterogeneous marginals, or dataset imbalance. Hence, even perfect redundancy
in the observed input does not guarantee symmetric DeepSHAP attributions.
∎

Lemma 2

(
State-Transition Smoothness
)
.

Let the recurrent update be given by

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle h_{t+1}=F(h_{t},x_{t+1})$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle h_{t+1}=F(h_{t},x_{t+1})$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle h_{t+1}=F(h_{t},x_{t+1})$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle h_{t+1}=F(h_{t},x_{t+1})$}}}.

Assume that
F
is
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle L_{h}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle L_{h}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle L_{h}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle L_{h}$}}}
-Lipschitz in its first argument and

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle L_{x}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle L_{x}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle L_{x}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle L_{x}$}}}
-Lipschitz in its second argument, i.e.,

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\|F(h,x)-F(h^{\prime},x^{\prime})\|\leq L_{h}\,\|h-h^{\prime}\|+L_{x}\,\|x-x^{\prime}\|$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\|F(h,x)-F(h^{\prime},x^{\prime})\|\leq L_{h}\,\|h-h^{\prime}\|+L_{x}\,\|x-x^{\prime}\|$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\|F(h,x)-F(h^{\prime},x^{\prime})\|\leq L_{h}\,\|h-h^{\prime}\|+L_{x}\,\|x-x^{\prime}\|$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\|F(h,x)-F(h^{\prime},x^{\prime})\|\leq L_{h}\,\|h-h^{\prime}\|+L_{x}\,\|x-x^{\prime}\|$}}}
for all
(h,x),(h^{\prime},x^{\prime}).

Define
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Delta_{t}:=\|h_{t+1}-h_{t}\|$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Delta_{t}:=\|h_{t+1}-h_{t}\|$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\Delta_{t}:=\|h_{t+1}-h_{t}\|$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta_{t}:=\|h_{t+1}-h_{t}\|$}}}
.
Then, for all
t\geq 1
,

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Delta_{t}\;\leq\;L_{h}\,\Delta_{t-1}+L_{x}\,\|x_{t+1}-x_{t}\|.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Delta_{t}\;\leq\;L_{h}\,\Delta_{t-1}+L_{x}\,\|x_{t+1}-x_{t}\|.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\Delta_{t}\;\leq\;L_{h}\,\Delta_{t-1}+L_{x}\,\|x_{t+1}-x_{t}\|.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta_{t}\;\leq\;L_{h}\,\Delta_{t-1}+L_{x}\,\|x_{t+1}-x_{t}\|.$}}}

Proof.

The goal is to bound the one-step hidden-state increment by separating the
contribution of changes in the hidden state from changes in the input. This
decoupling yields a recursive bound on the state differences.

Assume the recurrent update

h_{t+1}=F(h_{t},x_{t+1}),

(106)

and define

\Delta_{t}:=\|h_{t+1}-h_{t}\|.

(107)

Step 1 (Rewrite the increment using the update rule).

Using (
106
) at times
t
and
t-1
, we obtain

\Delta_{t}=\|F(h_{t},x_{t+1})-F(h_{t-1},x_{t})\|.

(108)

Step 2 (Insert an intermediate term).

Add and subtract
F(h_{t},x_{t})
and apply the triangle inequality:

\Delta_{t}\leq\|F(h_{t},x_{t+1})-F(h_{t},x_{t})\|+\|F(h_{t},x_{t})-F(h_{t-1},x_{t})\|.

(109)

Step 3 (Bound the input-only term).

By Lipschitz continuity of
F
in its second argument,

\|F(h_{t},x_{t+1})-F(h_{t},x_{t})\|\leq L_{x}\,\|x_{t+1}-x_{t}\|.

(110)

Step 4 (Bound the state-only term).

By Lipschitz continuity of
F
in its first argument,

\|F(h_{t},x_{t})-F(h_{t-1},x_{t})\|\leq L_{h}\,\|h_{t}-h_{t-1}\|=L_{h}\,\Delta_{t-1}.

(111)

Step 5 (Combine the bounds).

Substituting (
110
) and
(
111
) into (
109
) yields

\Delta_{t}\leq L_{h}\,\Delta_{t-1}+L_{x}\,\|x_{t+1}-x_{t}\|,

(112)

which is the desired state-transition smoothness bound.
∎

Theorem 36

(
Temporal Stability Bound for ERI-T
)
.

If the explanation map
E
is
L_{E}
-Lipschitz with respect to the hidden state,
i.e.,

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle d\!\left(E(x_{t}),E(x_{t+1})\right)\leq L_{E}\,\Delta_{t},$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle d\!\left(E(x_{t}),E(x_{t+1})\right)\leq L_{E}\,\Delta_{t},$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle d\!\left(E(x_{t}),E(x_{t+1})\right)\leq L_{E}\,\Delta_{t},$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle d\!\left(E(x_{t}),E(x_{t+1})\right)\leq L_{E}\,\Delta_{t},$}}}

then the temporal reliability score satisfies,
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathrm{ERI\text{-}T}\;\geq\;\left(1+\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{L_E}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{L_E}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{L_E}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{L_E}{T-1}}\mathop{\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sum$}}}}\displaylimits_{t=1}^{T-1}\Delta_{t}\right)^{-1}.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathrm{ERI\text{-}T}\;\geq\;\left(1+\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{L_E}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{L_E}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{L_E}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{L_E}{T-1}}\mathop{\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sum$}}}}\displaylimits_{t=1}^{T-1}\Delta_{t}\right)^{-1}.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathrm{ERI\text{-}T}\;\geq\;\left(1+\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{L_E}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{L_E}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{L_E}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{L_E}{T-1}}\mathop{\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sum$}}}}\displaylimits_{t=1}^{T-1}\Delta_{t}\right)^{-1}.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{ERI\text{-}T}\;\geq\;\left(1+\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{L_E}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{L_E}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{L_E}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{L_E}{T-1}}\mathop{\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sum$}}}}\displaylimits_{t=1}^{T-1}\Delta_{t}\right)^{-1}.$}}}

Proof.

The argument links temporal explanation drift to the underlying evolution of the
hidden representation driving the explainer. The key assumption is a Lipschitz
condition: explanations do not change faster than the hidden state.

Assume there exists a constant
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle L_{E}>0$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle L_{E}>0$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle L_{E}>0$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle L_{E}>0$}}}
such that for all consecutive
hidden states,

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle d\!\big(E(h_{t}),E(h_{t+1})\big)\;\leq\;L_{E}\,\|h_{t+1}-h_{t}\|=L_{E}\,\Delta_{t},$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle d\!\big(E(h_{t}),E(h_{t+1})\big)\;\leq\;L_{E}\,\|h_{t+1}-h_{t}\|=L_{E}\,\Delta_{t},$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle d\!\big(E(h_{t}),E(h_{t+1})\big)\;\leq\;L_{E}\,\|h_{t+1}-h_{t}\|=L_{E}\,\Delta_{t},$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle d\!\big(E(h_{t}),E(h_{t+1})\big)\;\leq\;L_{E}\,\|h_{t+1}-h_{t}\|=L_{E}\,\Delta_{t},$}}}

where
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Delta_{t}:=\|h_{t+1}-h_{t}\|$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Delta_{t}:=\|h_{t+1}-h_{t}\|$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\Delta_{t}:=\|h_{t+1}-h_{t}\|$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta_{t}:=\|h_{t+1}-h_{t}\|$}}}
.

In the time-series setting considered in the main text, explanations are
evaluated on the evolving state, i.e.,
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle E(x_{t})=E(h_{t})$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle E(x_{t})=E(h_{t})$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle E(x_{t})=E(h_{t})$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle E(x_{t})=E(h_{t})$}}}
. Hence, for each

t
,

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle d\!\big(E(x_{t}),E(x_{t+1})\big)\leq L_{E}\,\Delta_{t}.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle d\!\big(E(x_{t}),E(x_{t+1})\big)\leq L_{E}\,\Delta_{t}.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle d\!\big(E(x_{t}),E(x_{t+1})\big)\leq L_{E}\,\Delta_{t}.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle d\!\big(E(x_{t}),E(x_{t+1})\big)\leq L_{E}\,\Delta_{t}.$}}}

Define the temporal drift as

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle D_{T}:=\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{T-1}}\mathop{\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sum$}}}}\displaylimits_{t=1}^{T-1}d\!\big(E(x_{t}),E(x_{t+1})\big).$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle D_{T}:=\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{T-1}}\mathop{\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sum$}}}}\displaylimits_{t=1}^{T-1}d\!\big(E(x_{t}),E(x_{t+1})\big).$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle D_{T}:=\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{T-1}}\mathop{\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sum$}}}}\displaylimits_{t=1}^{T-1}d\!\big(E(x_{t}),E(x_{t+1})\big).$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle D_{T}:=\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{T-1}}\mathop{\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sum$}}}}\displaylimits_{t=1}^{T-1}d\!\big(E(x_{t}),E(x_{t+1})\big).$}}}

Substituting the per-step bound yields

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle D_{T}\;\leq\;\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{L_E}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{L_E}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{L_E}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{L_E}{T-1}}\mathop{\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sum$}}}}\displaylimits_{t=1}^{T-1}\Delta_{t}.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle D_{T}\;\leq\;\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{L_E}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{L_E}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{L_E}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{L_E}{T-1}}\mathop{\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sum$}}}}\displaylimits_{t=1}^{T-1}\Delta_{t}.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle D_{T}\;\leq\;\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{L_E}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{L_E}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{L_E}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{L_E}{T-1}}\mathop{\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sum$}}}}\displaylimits_{t=1}^{T-1}\Delta_{t}.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle D_{T}\;\leq\;\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{L_E}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{L_E}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{L_E}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{L_E}{T-1}}\mathop{\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sum$}}}}\displaylimits_{t=1}^{T-1}\Delta_{t}.$}}}

By definition, the temporal reliability score is

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathrm{ERI\text{-}T}=\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+D_T}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+D_T}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+D_T}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+D_T}}.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathrm{ERI\text{-}T}=\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+D_T}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+D_T}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+D_T}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+D_T}}.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathrm{ERI\text{-}T}=\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+D_T}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+D_T}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+D_T}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+D_T}}.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{ERI\text{-}T}=\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+D_T}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+D_T}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+D_T}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+D_T}}.$}}}

Combining the two inequalities gives

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathrm{ERI\text{-}T}\;\geq\;\left(1+\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{L_E}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{L_E}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{L_E}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{L_E}{T-1}}\mathop{\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sum$}}}}\displaylimits_{t=1}^{T-1}\Delta_{t}\right)^{-1},$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathrm{ERI\text{-}T}\;\geq\;\left(1+\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{L_E}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{L_E}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{L_E}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{L_E}{T-1}}\mathop{\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sum$}}}}\displaylimits_{t=1}^{T-1}\Delta_{t}\right)^{-1},$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathrm{ERI\text{-}T}\;\geq\;\left(1+\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{L_E}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{L_E}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{L_E}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{L_E}{T-1}}\mathop{\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sum$}}}}\displaylimits_{t=1}^{T-1}\Delta_{t}\right)^{-1},$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{ERI\text{-}T}\;\geq\;\left(1+\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{L_E}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{L_E}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{L_E}{T-1}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{L_E}{T-1}}\mathop{\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sum$}}}}\displaylimits_{t=1}^{T-1}\Delta_{t}\right)^{-1},$}}}

which completes the proof.
∎

Appendix I
Proofs of Main-Text Results

This section presents full, detailed proofs for all theoretical results
introduced in Section
5
. We use the same notation as in the main
paper:
f
is a predictive model,
E
is an explanation map,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
and
d(\cdot,\cdot)
is a valid distance metric. Throughout,
\|\cdot\|
denotes
an arbitrary norm (typically
\ell_{2}
), and we assume standard continuity and
Lipschitz properties for models and explainers.

I.1
Proof of Theorem
1
(Lipschitz Stability Bound)

Proof.

The statement asserts that if the predictive model responds smoothly to input
perturbations and the explanation map responds smoothly to changes in the model
output, then the resulting explanation drift is controlled linearly by the input
perturbation magnitude. The argument follows a simple transfer chain:

input perturbation

\rightarrow

model output change

\rightarrow

explanation drift
, followed by aggregation into the ERI
definition.

Step 1: Lipschitz propagation from input to model output.

By assumption, the predictive model

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}$}}}
is locally

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle L_{f}(x)$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle L_{f}(x)$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle L_{f}(x)$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle L_{f}(x)$}}}
-Lipschitz in a neighborhood of
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle x$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle x$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle x$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}}}
. Therefore, for
any perturbation
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\delta$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\delta$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\delta$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\delta$}}}
satisfying
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\|\delta\|\leq\epsilon$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\|\delta\|\leq\epsilon$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\|\delta\|\leq\epsilon$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\|\delta\|\leq\epsilon$}}}
,
we have

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\|f(x+\delta)-f(x)\|\;\leq\;L_{f}(x)\,\|\delta\|\;\leq\;L_{f}(x)\,\epsilon.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\|f(x+\delta)-f(x)\|\;\leq\;L_{f}(x)\,\|\delta\|\;\leq\;L_{f}(x)\,\epsilon.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\|f(x+\delta)-f(x)\|\;\leq\;L_{f}(x)\,\|\delta\|\;\leq\;L_{f}(x)\,\epsilon.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\|f(x+\delta)-f(x)\|\;\leq\;L_{f}(x)\,\|\delta\|\;\leq\;L_{f}(x)\,\epsilon.$}}}

Step 2: Lipschitz propagation from model output to explanation.

The explanation map

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle E:\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle E:\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle E:\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle E:\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}$}}}
is assumed to be

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle L_{E}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle L_{E}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle L_{E}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle L_{E}$}}}
-Lipschitz with respect to its input. Applying this property to
the pair
(f(x),f(x+\delta))
yields

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle d\!\big(E(f(x)),E(f(x+\delta))\big)\;\leq\;L_{E}\,\|f(x+\delta)-f(x)\|.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle d\!\big(E(f(x)),E(f(x+\delta))\big)\;\leq\;L_{E}\,\|f(x+\delta)-f(x)\|.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle d\!\big(E(f(x)),E(f(x+\delta))\big)\;\leq\;L_{E}\,\|f(x+\delta)-f(x)\|.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle d\!\big(E(f(x)),E(f(x+\delta))\big)\;\leq\;L_{E}\,\|f(x+\delta)-f(x)\|.$}}}

Step 3: Pointwise bound on explanation drift.

Combining the bounds from Steps 1 and 2 gives, for every admissible perturbation

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\delta$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\delta$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\delta$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\delta$}}}
,

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle d\!\big(E(f(x)),E(f(x+\delta))\big)\;\leq\;L_{E}\,L_{f}(x)\,\|\delta\|\;\leq\;L_{E}\,L_{f}(x)\,\epsilon.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle d\!\big(E(f(x)),E(f(x+\delta))\big)\;\leq\;L_{E}\,L_{f}(x)\,\|\delta\|\;\leq\;L_{E}\,L_{f}(x)\,\epsilon.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle d\!\big(E(f(x)),E(f(x+\delta))\big)\;\leq\;L_{E}\,L_{f}(x)\,\|\delta\|\;\leq\;L_{E}\,L_{f}(x)\,\epsilon.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle d\!\big(E(f(x)),E(f(x+\delta))\big)\;\leq\;L_{E}\,L_{f}(x)\,\|\delta\|\;\leq\;L_{E}\,L_{f}(x)\,\epsilon.$}}}

Step 4: Averaging over the perturbation law.

Let
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\delta$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\delta$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\delta$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\delta$}}}
be drawn from any perturbation distribution supported on

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\{\delta:\|\delta\|\leq\epsilon\}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\{\delta:\|\delta\|\leq\epsilon\}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\{\delta:\|\delta\|\leq\epsilon\}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\{\delta:\|\delta\|\leq\epsilon\}$}}}
. Since the pointwise bound holds
uniformly over the support, taking expectations preserves the inequality:

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Delta_{S}(x):=\mathbb{E}_{\delta}\!\left[d\!\big(E(f(x)),E(f(x+\delta))\big)\right]\;\leq\;L_{E}\,L_{f}(x)\,\epsilon.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Delta_{S}(x):=\mathbb{E}_{\delta}\!\left[d\!\big(E(f(x)),E(f(x+\delta))\big)\right]\;\leq\;L_{E}\,L_{f}(x)\,\epsilon.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\Delta_{S}(x):=\mathbb{E}_{\delta}\!\left[d\!\big(E(f(x)),E(f(x+\delta))\big)\right]\;\leq\;L_{E}\,L_{f}(x)\,\epsilon.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta_{S}(x):=\mathbb{E}_{\delta}\!\left[d\!\big(E(f(x)),E(f(x+\delta))\big)\right]\;\leq\;L_{E}\,L_{f}(x)\,\epsilon.$}}}

Step 5: Conversion to an ERI lower bound.

By definition, the perturbation-stability ERI component is

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathrm{ERI\mbox{-}S}(x)=\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_S(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_S(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+\Delta_S(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+\Delta_S(x)}}.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathrm{ERI\mbox{-}S}(x)=\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_S(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_S(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+\Delta_S(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+\Delta_S(x)}}.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathrm{ERI\mbox{-}S}(x)=\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_S(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_S(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+\Delta_S(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+\Delta_S(x)}}.$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{ERI\mbox{-}S}(x)=\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_S(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_S(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+\Delta_S(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+\Delta_S(x)}}.$}}}

Substituting the drift bound from Step 4 yields

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathrm{ERI\mbox{-}S}(x)\;\geq\;\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+L_E\,L_f(x)\,\epsilon}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+L_E\,L_f(x)\,\epsilon}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+L_E\,L_f(x)\,\epsilon}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+L_E\,L_f(x)\,\epsilon}},$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathrm{ERI\mbox{-}S}(x)\;\geq\;\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+L_E\,L_f(x)\,\epsilon}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+L_E\,L_f(x)\,\epsilon}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+L_E\,L_f(x)\,\epsilon}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+L_E\,L_f(x)\,\epsilon}},$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathrm{ERI\mbox{-}S}(x)\;\geq\;\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+L_E\,L_f(x)\,\epsilon}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+L_E\,L_f(x)\,\epsilon}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+L_E\,L_f(x)\,\epsilon}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+L_E\,L_f(x)\,\epsilon}},$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{ERI\mbox{-}S}(x)\;\geq\;\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+L_E\,L_f(x)\,\epsilon}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+L_E\,L_f(x)\,\epsilon}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+L_E\,L_f(x)\,\epsilon}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+L_E\,L_f(x)\,\epsilon}},$}}}

which completes the proof.
∎

Corollary 37

(Implication for ERI-R)
.

Under the conditions of Lemma
1
, the redundancy-collapse drift

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathrm{R}}(x)$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathrm{R}}(x)$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\Delta_{\mathrm{R}}(x)$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta_{\mathrm{R}}(x)$}}}
does not vanish as the redundancy parameter

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\alpha\to 1$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\alpha\to 1$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\alpha\to 1$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\alpha\to 1$}}}
. Consequently, the corresponding redundancy-stability
score satisfies

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathrm{ERI\mbox{-}R}(x)=\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_{\mathrm{R}}(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_{\mathrm{R}}(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+\Delta_{\mathrm{R}}(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+\Delta_{\mathrm{R}}(x)}}<1,$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathrm{ERI\mbox{-}R}(x)=\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_{\mathrm{R}}(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_{\mathrm{R}}(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+\Delta_{\mathrm{R}}(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+\Delta_{\mathrm{R}}(x)}}<1,$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathrm{ERI\mbox{-}R}(x)=\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_{\mathrm{R}}(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_{\mathrm{R}}(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+\Delta_{\mathrm{R}}(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+\Delta_{\mathrm{R}}(x)}}<1,$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{ERI\mbox{-}R}(x)=\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_{\mathrm{R}}(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_{\mathrm{R}}(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+\Delta_{\mathrm{R}}(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+\Delta_{\mathrm{R}}(x)}}<1,$}}}

i.e., ERI-R remains bounded away from perfect reliability.

I.2
Proof of Theorem
2
(Redundancy-Collapse Convergence)

Proof.

The claim links a
data-level
redundancy collapse (two coordinates becoming
asymptotically identical) to an
explanation-level
collapse, meaning that
the explanation produced before collapsing converges to the explanation obtained
after collapsing the redundant coordinates. The argument proceeds via continuity
and dominated convergence.

Step 1 (Redundancy forces coordinate convergence).

Start from the redundancy model

f_{j}=\alpha f_{i}+\sqrt{1-\alpha^{2}}\,Z,

(113)

where
Z
is zero-mean and independent of
f_{i}
. Subtracting
f_{i}
yields

f_{j}-f_{i}=(\alpha-1)f_{i}+\sqrt{1-\alpha^{2}}\,Z.

(114)

As
\alpha\to 1
, both coefficients vanish. Under mild moment assumptions
(e.g.,
\mathbb{E}[f_{i}^{2}]<\infty
and
\mathbb{E}[Z^{2}]<\infty
),

\mathbb{E}\big[(f_{j}-f_{i})^{2}\big]=(\alpha-1)^{2}\mathbb{E}[f_{i}^{2}]+(1-\alpha^{2})\mathbb{E}[Z^{2}]\;\xrightarrow[\alpha\to 1]{}\;0,

(115)

implying
f_{j}\to f_{i}
in probability (and almost surely along a subsequence).

Step 2 (Construct the redundant and collapsed inputs).

Let
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be an input whose
i
th and
j
th coordinates equal

f_{i}
and
f_{j}
, respectively. For each
\alpha
, define

x(\alpha):=\big(x_{1},\dots,x_{i-1},f_{i},x_{i+1},\dots,x_{j-1},f_{j}(\alpha),x_{j+1},\dots,x_{d}\big),

(116)

where
f_{j}(\alpha)
follows (
113
).
Let
x^{\mathrm{col}}
denote the input obtained by collapsing the redundant
pair
(i,j)
using the collapse operator associated with ERI-R, and define

E_{\mathrm{collapse}}(x):=E(x^{\mathrm{col}}).

(117)

Step 3 (Input convergence implies explanation convergence).

By Step 1, the only varying coordinate in
x(\alpha)
is the
j
th coordinate,
and
f_{j}(\alpha)\to f_{i}
in probability. Hence

\|x(\alpha)-x^{\star}\|\;\xrightarrow[\alpha\to 1]{}\;0,

(118)

where
x^{\star}
denotes the limiting input in which the
j
th coordinate equals
the
i
th coordinate. By continuity of the explainer
E
,

E\big(x(\alpha)\big)\;\xrightarrow[\alpha\to 1]{}\;E(x^{\star}).

(119)

By the assumed collapse consistency of
E
, this limit coincides with the
collapsed explanation,

E(x^{\star})=E(x^{\mathrm{col}})=E_{\mathrm{collapse}}(x).

(120)

Step 4 (Vanishing redundancy drift).

Since the distance
d(\cdot,\cdot)
is continuous,

d\!\Big(E\big(x(\alpha)\big),E_{\mathrm{collapse}}(x)\Big)\;\xrightarrow[\alpha\to 1]{}\;0.

(121)

Step 5 (Convergence of ERI-R).

Define the redundancy drift random variable

Y_{R}(\alpha):=d\!\Big(E\big(x(\alpha)\big),E_{\mathrm{collapse}}(x)\Big).

(122)

By (
121
),
Y_{R}(\alpha)\to 0
pointwise.
Assume
\{Y_{R}(\alpha)\}
is dominated by an integrable envelope (e.g., bounded
distance on a normalized explanation domain). By dominated convergence,

\Delta_{R}(x):=\mathbb{E}[Y_{R}(\alpha)]\;\xrightarrow[\alpha\to 1]{}\;0.

(123)

Since

\mathrm{ERI\mbox{-}R}(x)=\mathchoice{\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}$}}}}{\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}$}}}}{\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}}{\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1+\Delta_R(x)}}},

(124)

we conclude

\lim_{\alpha\to 1}\mathrm{ERI\mbox{-}R}(x)=1,

(125)

which completes the proof.
∎

Appendix J
Axiomatic Justification: Representation and Minimality

In the main text, we introduced ERI as a reliability score that measures how
stable an explanation map is under controlled, non-adversarial variations. In
this appendix, we justify this choice axiomatically. The goal is not merely to
show that ERI is
a
reasonable reliability score, but to show that it is

canonical
: once we accept a small set of reliability axioms together
with mild regularity requirements, any admissible reliability functional must
collapse to a monotone transformation of an
expected explanation drift
.
This mirrors the logic of classical representation results (e.g., expected
utility and Shapley-style characterizations): the axioms restrict the space of
admissible functionals so strongly that only a single family remains.

We proceed in two parts. First, we prove a representation theorem. Second, we
show minimality: each axiom is independent, in the sense that removing any one
axiom allows pathological reliability scores that ERI excludes.

J.1
Reliability functionals and regularity

Definition 23

(
Reliability Functional
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{D}
denote a family of admissible transformation laws, where a
draw
\omega\sim\mathcal{D}\in\mathfrak{D}
specifies a transformation

\mathcal{T}_{\omega}
applied to an input
x
(e.g., input perturbation, feature
collapse, checkpoint update, or distributional shift). A
reliability
functional
is any mapping

R:\;\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathfrak{D}\to[0,1],

(126)

which assigns to each input
x
and transformation law
\mathcal{D}
a scalar
quantifying the reliability of the explanation
E(x)
under the induced
variation.

Definition 24

(
Regularity Conditions
)
.

A reliability functional
R
satisfies the following mild regularity
assumptions.

•

Monotonicity.

For any fixed
x
and any two perturbations
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}
(drawn from any
admissible laws), if the induced explanation drift is smaller under
\omega_{1}

than under
\omega_{2}
, then reliability under
\omega_{1}
is at least as high as
under
\omega_{2}
. Formally, with
x^{\prime}=\mathcal{T}_{\omega}(x)
,

d\!\Big(E(x),E(\mathcal{T}_{\omega_{1}}(x))\Big)\leq d\!\Big(E(x),E(\mathcal{T}_{\omega_{2}}(x))\Big)\quad\Longrightarrow\quad R(x,\omega_{1})\geq R(x,\omega_{2}).

(127)

•

Continuity.

If a sequence of transformation laws
\mathcal{D}_{n}
converges weakly to

\mathcal{D}
, then the reliability score varies continuously:

\mathcal{D}_{n}\Rightarrow\mathcal{D}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad R(x,\mathcal{D}_{n})\to R(x,\mathcal{D}).

(128)

Monotonicity encodes the most basic ordering principle: if an explanation
changes less, it should not be deemed less reliable. Continuity rules out
scores that jump discontinuously under infinitesimal changes in the
transformation law (e.g., tiny shifts in perturbation variance or background
distribution).

J.2
Representation theorem: reliability must be a monotone transform of expected drift

Theorem 38

(
Representation Theorem (Admissible Class and Canonical ERI Choice)
)
.

Let
R
be a reliability functional that satisfies the four reliability axioms
from the main text:
(i) perturbation stability (Axiom 1),
(ii) redundancy–collapse consistency (Axiom 2),
(iii) model-evolution consistency (Axiom 3),
(iv) distributional robustness (Axiom 4),
together with the regularity conditions in
Definition
24
(monotonicity and continuity).
Fix an input
x
and let
\mathcal{D}\in\mathfrak{D}
be an admissible law over
transformations/perturbations. Define the induced drift random variable

Y(\omega)\;:=\;d\!\big(E(x),\,E(\mathcal{T}_{\omega}(x))\big),\qquad\omega\sim\mathcal{D}.

(129)

Then
R(x,\mathcal{D})
belongs to the following representation class: there
exist

(i)

a scalar summary
\mathcal{S}(x,\mathcal{D})\in[0,\infty)
that depends on

\mathcal{D}

only through the law of
Y
, and

(ii)

a continuous, strictly decreasing function
\psi:[0,\infty)\to[0,1]

such that

R(x,\mathcal{D})\;=\;\psi\!\big(\mathcal{S}(x,\mathcal{D})\big).

(130)

Moreover, the
mean drift

D(x,\mathcal{D})\;:=\;\mathbb{E}_{\omega\sim\mathcal{D}}\!\big[Y(\omega)\big]=\mathbb{E}_{\omega\sim\mathcal{D}}\Big[d\!\big(E(x),E(\mathcal{T}_{\omega}(x))\big)\Big]

(131)

is a particularly natural and stable choice of
\mathcal{S}(x,\mathcal{D})
,
and a canonical ERI instance can be obtained via, for example,

\mathrm{ERI}(x,\mathcal{D})\;:=\;\mathchoice{\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1 + D(x,\mathcal{D})}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1 + D(x,\mathcal{D})}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1 + D(x,\mathcal{D})}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1 + D(x,\mathcal{D})}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1 + D(x,\mathcal{D})}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1 + D(x,\mathcal{D})}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1 + D(x,\mathcal{D})}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1 + D(x,\mathcal{D})}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1 + D(x,\mathcal{D})}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1 + D(x,\mathcal{D})}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1 + D(x,\mathcal{D})}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1 + D(x,\mathcal{D})}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1 + D(x,\mathcal{D})}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1 + D(x,\mathcal{D})}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1 + D(x,\mathcal{D})}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1 + D(x,\mathcal{D})}}$}}}}{\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1 + D(x,\mathcal{D})}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1 + D(x,\mathcal{D})}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1 + D(x,\mathcal{D})}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1 + D(x,\mathcal{D})}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1 + D(x,\mathcal{D})}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1 + D(x,\mathcal{D})}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1 + D(x,\mathcal{D})}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1 + D(x,\mathcal{D})}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1 + D(x,\mathcal{D})}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1 + D(x,\mathcal{D})}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1 + D(x,\mathcal{D})}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1 + D(x,\mathcal{D})}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1 + D(x,\mathcal{D})}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1 + D(x,\mathcal{D})}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1 + D(x,\mathcal{D})}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1 + D(x,\mathcal{D})}}$}}}}{\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1 + D(x,\mathcal{D})}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1 + D(x,\mathcal{D})}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1 + D(x,\mathcal{D})}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1 + D(x,\mathcal{D})}}}{\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1 + D(x,\mathcal{D})}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{1 + D(x,\mathcal{D})}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{1 + D(x,\mathcal{D})}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{1 + D(x,\mathcal{D})}}}\in(0,1].

(132)

Importantly, the theorem does not claim that the axioms uniquely force the
expectation operator.
Rather, the axioms and regularity conditions restrict

R
to the admissible class (
130
), within which
(
131
) provides a canonical, robust choice.

Proof.

The proof proceeds in three stages:

(a) realization-level sufficiency
(Axiom 1),

(b) monotone scalarization
(regularity),
and
(c) law-level dependence only through the induced drift law

(Axiom 4 + continuity). We then justify (not derive uniquely) the mean drift as
a canonical choice using Axioms 2–3.

Step 1: Realization-level sufficiency (dependence only on drift magnitude).

Fix
x
. Consider two realizations
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}
such that they induce the
same drift magnitude:

d\!\big(E(x),E(\mathcal{T}_{\omega_{1}}(x))\big)=d\!\big(E(x),E(\mathcal{T}_{\omega_{2}}(x))\big).

(133)

By perturbation stability (Axiom 1), indistinguishable explanation changes must
receive identical reliability scores at the realization level:

R(x,\omega_{1})=R(x,\omega_{2}).

(134)

Hence, for each fixed
x
, there exists a function

\phi_{x}:[0,\infty)\to[0,1]
such that for every realization
\omega
,

R(x,\omega)=\phi_{x}\!\Big(d\!\big(E(x),E(\mathcal{T}_{\omega}(x))\big)\Big)=\phi_{x}\!\big(Y(\omega)\big).

(135)

Thus, once the drift magnitude is fixed, the identity of
\omega
carries no
additional admissible information for reliability.

Step 2: Monotonicity implies
\phi_{x}
is decreasing.

By monotonicity (Definition
24
), larger drift
cannot yield higher reliability. Therefore, for any
y_{1}\leq y_{2}
,

y_{1}\leq y_{2}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\phi_{x}(y_{1})\geq\phi_{x}(y_{2}),

(136)

i.e.,
\phi_{x}
is non-increasing (and strictly decreasing on any range where
different drifts occur with positive probability).

Step 3: Law-level dependence only through the induced drift law.

We now consider a law
\mathcal{D}
over realizations
\omega
and the induced
drift random variable
Y(\omega)
from (
129
).
Since (
135
) shows that
R
depends on
\omega
only through

Y(\omega)
, any law-level score
R(x,\mathcal{D})
can depend on
\mathcal{D}

only through the distribution of
Y
.

Distributional robustness (Axiom 4) together with continuity implies that small
changes in
\mathcal{D}
(in the sense of the probability metric specified in
Axiom 4) produce small changes in
R(x,\mathcal{D})
, and that relabelings of
realizations that preserve the law of
Y
do not affect the score. Therefore,
there exists a scalar functional
\mathcal{S}(x,\mathcal{D})\in[0,\infty)
,
depending on
\mathcal{D}
only through the law of
Y
, and a continuous,
strictly decreasing
\psi:[0,\infty)\to[0,1]
such that

R(x,\mathcal{D})=\psi\!\big(\mathcal{S}(x,\mathcal{D})\big).

(137)

This establishes the admissible representation class
(
130
).
Note that Step 3 does not assert that

\mathcal{S}
must be an expectation, nor does it require mixture-linearity.

Step 4: Canonical choice (mean drift) and ERI normalization.

Equation (
137
) permits multiple admissible summaries

\mathcal{S}(x,\mathcal{D})
(e.g., mean, median, trimmed mean, or other
law-invariant continuous functionals of
Y
). We now justify why the mean drift
(
131
) is a particularly natural and stable choice under the
reliability axioms, without claiming uniqueness.

Axiom 2 (redundancy–collapse consistency).

Redundancy collapse corresponds to a symmetry-preserving reparameterization in a
lower-dimensional representation. Summaries that depend strongly on
fine-grained shape features of the drift distribution (e.g., certain tail or
quantile-based summaries) can vary under such reparameterizations even when the
average explanation displacement is preserved. The mean drift
D(x,\mathcal{D})

is invariant to such symmetry-preserving reparameterizations whenever the drift
distribution is preserved up to measure-preserving transformations.

Axiom 3 (model-evolution consistency).

Under smooth model evolution, explanation drift should vary smoothly.
Many alternative summaries (e.g., discontinuous threshold-based scores or some
quantile/tail functionals) can change abruptly under arbitrarily small
perturbations of the drift law, whereas the mean drift varies continuously
whenever
Y
changes continuously in distribution and is uniformly integrable.

Thus, Axioms 2–3 favor summaries that are symmetric under redundancy-induced
reparameterizations and stable under smooth evolution. The mean drift is a
simple canonical choice satisfying these desiderata. Finally, selecting a
strictly decreasing continuous rescaling such as
\psi(t)=1/(1+t)
yields the
bounded and interpretable ERI in (
132
).
∎

Axiom / Assumption

What it rules out

Why it matters for ERI

A1: Perturbation stability

Discontinuous “jumps” in explanations under vanishing input noise

Forces local robustness and prevents brittleness

A2: Redundancy-collapse consistency

Arbitrary favoritism among redundant/correlated features

Enforces symmetry and dependence-awareness

A3: Model-evolution consistency

Explanation oscillations across near-identical checkpoints

Enables longitudinal monitoring and debugging

A4: Distributional robustness

Discontinuous changes under small dataset/environment shifts

Ensures deployment reliability under mild drift

Monotonicity

Scores increasing when drift increases

Aligns the score with the intended notion of stability

Continuity

Scores that jump under small perturbation-law changes

Enables stable benchmarking and reproducibility

Table 5
:
Role of axioms and regularity assumptions in the ERI characterization.

Appendix K
Minimality of the Axioms

A representation theorem is only compelling if its axioms are not redundant.
We therefore show that each axiom contributes independent content: removing any
one axiom admits a reliability functional that satisfies the remaining three
axioms and the regularity conditions, yet is not equivalent to ERI.
This establishes that the axiom set is minimal.

Proposition 39

(Minimality of Axioms)
.

Each of the four axioms—perturbation stability (A1), redundancy-collapse
consistency (A2), model-evolution consistency (A3), and distributional robustness
(A4)—is independent.
That is, for every axiom
A_{k}
there exists a reliability functional
R^{(k)}

satisfying the other three axioms but violating
A_{k}
.

We construct explicit counterexamples by starting from a baseline explanation
map
E_{\mathrm{std}}
that already satisfies all axioms (e.g., ERI applied to a
stable explainer), and then injecting a carefully designed perturbation term
that breaks exactly one axiom while leaving the others intact.

Proof.

Throughout, let
E_{\mathrm{std}}
denote an explanation map that satisfies
Axioms A1–A4. For each
k\in\{1,2,3,4\}
, construct a modified explanation map

E^{(k)}
that intentionally violates exactly one axiom while preserving the
other three. To keep the comparison consistent across cases, define the
associated reliability functional using the same ERI-style form

R^{(k)}(x,\mathcal{D}):=1-\mathbb{E}_{\delta\sim\mathcal{D}}\Big[d\!\big(E^{(k)}(x),E^{(k)}(x+\delta)\big)\Big].

(138)

The goal is purely logical: exhibit
existence
of pathologies, not propose
alternative reliability scores.

Case 1: Dropping Axiom A1 (Perturbation Stability).

Construction.

Fix
K>0
and define, for every perturbation
\delta
,

E^{(1)}(x+\delta)=E_{\mathrm{std}}(x+\delta)+K\,\mathrm{sign}(\delta),

(139)

where
\mathrm{sign}(\delta)
is applied coordinatewise and takes values in

\{-1,0,1\}^{d}
. Also set
E^{(1)}(x)=E_{\mathrm{std}}(x)
(equivalently, interpret
(
139
) with
\delta=0
so the extra term vanishes).

Why A1 fails.

Axiom A1 requires that as
\|\delta\|\to 0
, the explanation drift

d(E(x),E(x+\delta))
must vanish. Here the added term in (
139
) does
not vanish with
\delta
whenever the sign pattern stays fixed. For instance,
take
\delta=\varepsilon e_{1}
with
\varepsilon>0
and
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
,
so
\mathrm{sign}(\delta)=e_{1}
. Then

E^{(1)}(x+\varepsilon e_{1})-E^{(1)}(x)=\Big(E_{\mathrm{std}}(x+\varepsilon e_{1})-E_{\mathrm{std}}(x)\Big)+Ke_{1}.

(140)

If
d
dominates the coordinatewise absolute difference (as is the case for any

\ell_{p}
metric or any metric lower-bounding
\|\,\cdot\,\|
), then

d\!\big(E^{(1)}(x),E^{(1)}(x+\varepsilon e_{1})\big)\geq\|Ke_{1}\|-\big\|E_{\mathrm{std}}(x+\varepsilon e_{1})-E_{\mathrm{std}}(x)\big\|.

(141)

Since
E_{\mathrm{std}}
is perturbation-stable, the second term goes to
0
as

\varepsilon\downarrow 0
, hence the right-hand side tends to
\|Ke_{1}\|>0
.
Therefore the drift does
not
vanish as
\|\delta\|\to 0
, violating A1.

Why A2–A4 still hold.

A2 (redundancy-collapse consistency) concerns symmetry with respect to redundant
feature identities. The additive term in (
139
) depends only on the
perturbation vector
\delta
and treats coordinates uniformly through the same

\mathrm{sign}(\cdot)
operation; it does not introduce a preference between
redundant coordinates beyond what is already present in
E_{\mathrm{std}}
.
A3 (model-evolution consistency) concerns smooth dependence on the model
trajectory. The new term is independent of model parameters and therefore does
not inject additional checkpoint-induced drift beyond
E_{\mathrm{std}}
.
A4 (distributional robustness) concerns stability under weak changes in

\mathcal{D}
. Under mild integrability, weak convergence

\mathcal{D}_{n}\Rightarrow\mathcal{D}
preserves expectations of bounded
measurable functions such as
\mathrm{sign}(\delta)
away from pathological mass
concentrations; thus the distributional behavior remains continuous at the level
required in A4.

Case 2: Dropping Axiom A2 (Redundancy-Collapse Consistency).

Construction.

Fix
\eta>0
and a deterministic non-symmetric vector
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and set

E^{(2)}(x)=E_{\mathrm{std}}(x)+\eta v.

(142)

For concreteness, if
(i,j)
is a redundant pair, choose
v
such that
v_{i}=1

and
v_{j}=0
.

Why A2 fails.

A2 requires that if two features are perfectly redundant (so the data manifold
collapses along
x_{i}=x_{j}
), the explanation must not systematically privilege
one over the other once the collapse rule is applied. Under (
142
),
for the redundant pair
(i,j)
,

E^{(2)}_{i}(x)-E^{(2)}_{j}(x)=\Big(E_{\mathrm{std},i}(x)-E_{\mathrm{std},j}(x)\Big)+\eta(v_{i}-v_{j}).

(143)

Even if
E_{\mathrm{std}}
satisfies redundancy-collapse consistency (so the
first difference is
0
in the redundant limit), the bias term forces

E^{(2)}_{i}(x)-E^{(2)}_{j}(x)=\eta\neq 0,

(144)

so the explainer remains asymmetric under perfect redundancy. Hence A2 is
violated by construction.

Why A1, A3, A4 still hold.

A1 concerns how
d(E(x),E(x+\delta))
behaves for small
\delta
. Since the bias

\eta v
is constant in
x
, it cancels in differences:

E^{(2)}(x+\delta)-E^{(2)}(x)=E_{\mathrm{std}}(x+\delta)-E_{\mathrm{std}}(x).

(145)

Therefore perturbation stability is inherited directly from
E_{\mathrm{std}}
.
A3 is preserved because the same constant bias is added at every checkpoint; it
does not introduce oscillations across model evolution. A4 is preserved because
the modification is independent of
\mathcal{D}
, so weak changes in

\mathcal{D}
affect
R^{(2)}
only through the original
E_{\mathrm{std}}
term.

Case 3: Dropping Axiom A3 (Model-Evolution Consistency).

Construction.

Let
t
index successive checkpoints
\theta_{t}
. Choose a nonzero vector

u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and define a checkpoint-dependent explainer by

E^{(3)}_{t}(x)=E_{\mathrm{std}}(x)+(-1)^{t}u.

(146)

Why A3 fails.

A3 demands that when the model parameters change smoothly along the trajectory,
the explanations should not exhibit unrelated abrupt jumps. However,
(
146
) yields a deterministic sign flip every step:

E^{(3)}_{t+1}(x)-E^{(3)}_{t}(x)=\Big(E_{\mathrm{std}}(x)-E_{\mathrm{std}}(x)\Big)+\big((-1)^{t+1}-(-1)^{t}\big)u=-2(-1)^{t}u.

(147)

Thus the explanation drift across consecutive checkpoints is lower-bounded by a
constant proportional to
\|u\|
, independent of how small

\|\theta_{t+1}-\theta_{t}\|
is. This violates model-evolution consistency.

Why A1, A2, A4 still hold.

For a fixed checkpoint
t
, the additive term
(-1)^{t}u
is constant in
x
and

\delta
, so it cancels in perturbation differences:

E^{(3)}_{t}(x+\delta)-E^{(3)}_{t}(x)=E_{\mathrm{std}}(x+\delta)-E_{\mathrm{std}}(x).

(148)

Hence A1 is inherited from
E_{\mathrm{std}}
. A2 is preserved because the
added vector does not privilege redundant features; it is an equal offset in
the explanation space not tied to any feature identity. A4 is preserved for the
same reason as in Case 2: the modification does not depend on
\mathcal{D}
, so
distributional continuity is unchanged for each fixed
t
.

Case 4: Dropping Axiom A4 (Distributional Robustness).

Construction.

Let
\tau>0
and
w\neq 0
be fixed. Define an explainer that depends
discontinuously on the perturbation distribution
\mathcal{D}
:

E^{(4)}_{\mathcal{D}}(x)=E_{\mathrm{std}}(x)+\mathbf{1}\!\left\{\mathbb{E}_{\delta\sim\mathcal{D}}\big[\|\delta\|\big]>\tau\right\}w.

(149)

Why A4 fails.

A4 requires that small changes in the perturbation law should not cause abrupt
changes in reliability. Consider a sequence
\{\mathcal{D}_{n}\}
such that

\mathcal{D}_{n}\Rightarrow\mathcal{D}
and

\mathbb{E}_{\delta\sim\mathcal{D}_{n}}\big[\|\delta\|\big]\downarrow\tau\qquad\text{while}\qquad\mathbb{E}_{\delta\sim\mathcal{D}}\big[\|\delta\|\big]=\tau.

(150)

For all large
n
with expectation strictly larger than
\tau
, the indicator in
(
149
) equals
1
, whereas at the limit distribution it equals
0
.
Hence
E^{(4)}_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}(x)
differs from
E^{(4)}_{\mathcal{D}}(x)
by the
non-vanishing offset
w
for arbitrarily close distributions, producing a
discontinuity in reliability as a function of
\mathcal{D}
. This violates A4.
A concrete instantiation is
\delta\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}I)
, where

\mathbb{E}\|\delta\|
varies continuously with
\sigma
, yet the indicator
creates a jump at the first
\sigma
that crosses the threshold
\tau
.

Why A1–A3 still hold.

For any fixed
\mathcal{D}
, the indicator is a constant (either
0
or
1
),
hence it cancels in perturbation differences:

E^{(4)}_{\mathcal{D}}(x+\delta)-E^{(4)}_{\mathcal{D}}(x)=E_{\mathrm{std}}(x+\delta)-E_{\mathrm{std}}(x),

(151)

so A1 is inherited from
E_{\mathrm{std}}
. The added term is symmetric across
features (it is a uniform offset in explanation space), so A2 is unaffected. It
is also independent of model checkpoints, so A3 is unaffected.

Each construction produces a concrete explainer
E^{(k)}
(hence a reliability
functional via (
138
)) that violates exactly one axiom while
preserving the other three. Therefore none of the axioms is implied by the
remaining three. Each axiom eliminates a distinct failure mode, and the set of
axioms is independent and minimal.
∎

Drop

Counterexample modification

Pathology introduced

Intuition

A1

+K\cdot\mathrm{sign}(\delta)

finite jumps under
\|\delta\|\to 0

brittle explanation under tiny noise

A2

+\eta v
(non-symmetric
v
)

deterministic favoritism among redundant features

violates redundancy symmetry

A3

+(-1)^{t}u

oscillations across checkpoints

breaks longitudinal consistency

A4

+\mathbf{1}\{\mathbb{E}\|\delta\|>\tau\}w

discontinuous response to small distribution shift

unstable under mild drift

Table 6
:
Summary of minimality constructions: each dropped axiom admits a
counterexample producing a distinct failure mode.

Appendix L
Sample Complexity of ERI Estimation

All ERI scores are computed empirically using Monte Carlo sampling over
perturbations, redundancy realizations, temporal indices, or model checkpoints.
Without finite-sample guarantees, it would be unclear whether observed ERI
differences reflect genuine explanation reliability or merely sampling noise.
This section establishes concentration bounds and sample-complexity guarantees
for ERI estimation, demonstrating that ERI can be estimated accurately and
efficiently in practice. We first restate ERI as a Monte Carlo estimator of an expected explanation drift.
We then derive a finite-sample concentration inequality using Hoeffding’s bound,
translate it into an explicit sample-complexity requirement, and conclude with
practical interpretation and numerical guidance.
Let
\delta\sim\mathcal{D}
denote a random perturbation (or, more generally,
a draw from the transformation distribution defining a given ERI component).
Define the explanation drift random variable

Z_{\delta}:=d\!\left(E(x),E(x+\delta)\right),

and assume that
Z_{\delta}
is bounded almost surely:

0\leq Z_{\delta}\leq 1.

This assumption holds automatically when using cosine distance,
clamped distances, or normalized attribution vectors, as enforced by ERI-Bench.

Given
n
i.i.d. samples
\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{n}\sim\mathcal{D}
, the empirical
ERI estimator is

\widehat{\mathrm{ERI}}_{n}(x):=1-\mathchoice{\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}}$}}}}{\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}}$}}}}{\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}}}{\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}}}\mathop{\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sum$}}}}\displaylimits_{k=1}^{n}Z_{\delta_{k}},

while the population ERI is

\mathrm{ERI}(x)=1-\mathbb{E}_{\delta\sim\mathcal{D}}\big[Z_{\delta}\big].

Theorem 40

(Monte Carlo Convergence of ERI)
.

Assume
0\leq Z_{\delta}\leq 1
almost surely.
Then for any
\eta>0
,

\Pr\!\left(\big|\widehat{\mathrm{ERI}}_{n}(x)-\mathrm{ERI}(x)\big|\geq\eta\right)\leq 2\exp(-2n\eta^{2}).

Proof.

We proceed step by step.

Step 1: Express ERI estimation error as a mean deviation.

Define the empirical mean of the drift:

\overline{Z}_{n}:=\mathchoice{\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}}$}}}}{\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}}$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}}$}}}}{\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}}}{\mathchoice{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{0}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{1}{1}{n}}{\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}}}\mathop{\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\textstyle\sum$}}{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sum$}}}}\displaylimits_{k=1}^{n}Z_{\delta_{k}}.

By definition,

\widehat{\mathrm{ERI}}_{n}(x)=1-\overline{Z}_{n},\qquad\mathrm{ERI}(x)=1-\mathbb{E}[Z_{\delta}].

Therefore,

\widehat{\mathrm{ERI}}_{n}(x)-\mathrm{ERI}(x)=\mathbb{E}[Z_{\delta}]-\overline{Z}_{n},

and hence

\big|\widehat{\mathrm{ERI}}_{n}(x)-\mathrm{ERI}(x)\big|=\big|\overline{Z}_{n}-\mathbb{E}[Z_{\delta}]\big|.

Thus bounding the ERI estimation error reduces exactly to bounding the deviation
of a sample mean from its expectation.

Step 2: Apply Hoeffding’s inequality.

Since
Z_{\delta_{1}},\dots,Z_{\delta_{n}}
are i.i.d. and almost surely bounded in

[0,1]
, Hoeffding’s inequality yields

\Pr\!\left(\Big|\overline{Z}_{n}-\mathbb{E}[Z_{\delta}]\Big|\geq\eta\right)\leq 2\exp(-2n\eta^{2}).

Step 3: Translate back to ERI.

Using the identity from Step 1, we obtain

\Pr\!\left(\big|\widehat{\mathrm{ERI}}_{n}(x)-\mathrm{ERI}(x)\big|\geq\eta\right)\leq 2\exp(-2n\eta^{2}),

which completes the proof.
∎

(Sample Complexity of ERI Estimation)
.

it suffices to choose

,

Multiplying by
-1
and rearranging yields

Corollary
41
shows that ERI estimation enjoys the
standard Monte Carlo rate:

n=O\!\left(\mathchoice{\mathord{\mathchoice{\raise This has several important implications:

•

Statistical feasibility.

Accurate ERI estimation does not require large sample sizes.
For example, achieving
\eta=0.05
accuracy with
95\%
confidence requires
only
n\approx 600
perturbations.

•

Scalability.

The bound is independent of input dimension
d
and model size; it depends
only on the desired accuracy and confidence.

•

Comparability across explainers.

Since all explainers are evaluated under the same
n
and distance bounds,
ERI differences are statistically meaningful rather than sampling artifacts.
